Session 1: Toolkit and First Arrays

Your working notebook for the first session of Python Data Science. First you check that your computer is ready and warm up your Python. Then you put the toolkit to work on 20,302 orders from Pinecrest Outfitters, and meet the NumPy array: the structure underneath pandas, Matplotlib and nearly everything else in this course.

Lessons 0.1–1.1 Session 1 · 3 hours Name: ____________________
How to use this workbook

Each lesson has By the end you can (your goals), Key ideas (the page to keep), a Worked example (new code with its real output), the Guided lab you do in class, Predict, then run items, the lesson project (started in class, finished at home), a six-question Self-check and the Journal prompt. Lab files are in labs/module-01/: open the _starter.py file, never the solution, and run it once before you change anything. Write in this workbook. Bring it every session.

Session Goals

  • Show that your computer is ready: check_environment.py ends with READY for Python Data Science.
  • Run code in a notebook and as a script, and get back to a working (.venv) prompt without help.
  • Load a real file with a relative path, pick a column, count its values and filter its rows.
  • Read a traceback from the bottom and take a sensible next step.
  • Create NumPy arrays, read their dtype and shape, and select rows, columns and cells.
  • Predict when a slice is a view, and make a copy when you need one.
  • Start both lesson projects: first_look.py and the list-versus-array benchmark report.

0.1

Your Data Science Toolkit

By the end you can
  • Install Python 3.12 or newer and create a virtual environment for the course on Windows, macOS or Linux
  • Install the course packages from a requirements.txt file and confirm they work
  • Run code both in a notebook and as a script, and say when each one suits the job
  • Download the course data and load a file from it with a relative path
  • Read a Python traceback from the bottom up and take a sensible next step when you are stuck
Key ideas
  • One project, one environment. .venv holds this course's packages and nothing else. Activate it in every new terminal and look for (.venv).
  • Install once, from the file. python -m pip install -r requirements.txt. The ~= pins keep you on the versions the lessons were tested with.
  • Notebook to explore, script to keep. A notebook remembers variables between cells; a script starts fresh and shows only what you print().
  • Relative paths start where Python is working. "data/orders.csv" only works when you start from the project folder.
  • Three pandas moves for today: read a file, pick a column with square brackets, then count (value_counts()), summarize (describe()) or filter (orders[condition]).
  • Read a traceback from the bottom. Last line: what went wrong. Then find the line in your own file.
  • An unusual value is a question, not automatically a mistake.

Worked example: who pays for shipping?

A new question for the three moves. Shipping is charged on some online orders. How many orders paid each amount, and what share of online orders shipped free?

import pandas as pd

orders = pd.read_csv("data/orders.csv", parse_dates=["order_date"])
print(orders["shipping_cost"].value_counts())
shipping_cost
0.00     16624
6.95      2754
14.95      924
Name: count, dtype: int64

Three shipping prices: free, $6.95 and $14.95. But 16,624 free orders is more than all the online orders there are, because store orders never have shipping. The question is about online orders, so filter first.

online = orders[orders["channel"] == "online"]
free = online["shipping_cost"] == 0

print(len(online), "online orders")
print(free.sum(), "shipped free:", round(free.mean() * 100, 1), "percent")
12854 online orders
9176 shipped free: 71.4 percent

free is a column of True and False. Its .sum() counts the True values and its .mean() is the fraction that is True. Read the result as a sentence: about 71% of online orders shipped free.

1.1

Arrays vs Lists

By the end you can
  • Explain how a NumPy array stores numbers differently from a Python list, and why that makes it smaller and faster
  • Create arrays from lists, from scratch, and from a CSV file, and read their dtype, shape, ndim and size
  • Select elements, rows and columns with indexing and slicing in one and two dimensions
  • Predict whether an operation gives you a view or a copy, and make a copy when you need one
  • Measure memory and speed yourself and choose between a list and an array for a task
Key ideas
  • A list is an address book; an array is one block of values. The list keeps an address for every item and every item is a full Python object. The array stores the type once, then only the numbers.
  • Three rules: one type for the whole array (the dtype), a fixed size, and operations that act on every element at once.
  • *** 2 doubles an array; it repeats a list.** The same is true for +: arrays add element by element, lists join.
  • Mixed values are converted silently. One text value makes the whole array text. A decimal stored into an integer array is chopped. When in doubt, print .dtype.
  • Rows, comma, columns. table[row, col]; a lone : means all of them. Print .shape when you are not sure which is which.
  • A slice is a window, not a photocopy. Writing to a slice changes the original. .copy() gives you an independent array; np.shares_memory(a, b) settles any doubt.
  • Arrays do not grow. Collect in a list, convert once, compute on the array.
  • **nan means a missing value got into the calculation.** np.isnan(x).sum() counts them; np.nanmean, np.nansum, np.nanmax and np.nanmin skip them.
You want to...Write
Make an array from a listweek = np.array(week_list)
Describe an arrayweek.dtype, week.shape, week.ndim, week.size, week.nbytes
Build one from scratchnp.zeros(5), np.arange(0, 10, 2), np.linspace(0, 1, 5)
Load one CSV columnnp.genfromtxt("data/daily_sales.csv", delimiter=",", skip_header=1, usecols=5)
Change the type (new array)counts.astype(np.float64)
One cell, one row, one columnt[0, 2], t[1], t[:, 2]
Give 28 values a table shaperevenue[:28].reshape(7, 4)
An independent copya[1:4].copy()
Mean that skips missing valuesnp.nanmean(revenue)
Worked example: two years of high temperatures as a table

data/store_weather.csv has one row per city per day: 731 days for each of four cities, sorted by city (Boise, Bend, Flagstaff, Asheville), then by date. Column 2 is the day's high in °F. Because every city has the same number of rows and nothing is missing, the whole column fits a 4 by 731 table.

import numpy as np

high = np.genfromtxt("data/store_weather.csv", delimiter=",", skip_header=1, usecols=2)
print(high.shape, high.dtype, np.isnan(high).sum())
(2924,) float64 0

2,924 values, all float64 (that is what genfromtxt gives unless told otherwise), none missing.

temps = high.reshape(4, 731)        # rows = cities, columns = days
print(temps.shape)
print(temps[0, :7])                 # Boise, first week of January 2024
print(temps[:, 0])                  # every city on 1 January 2024
(4, 731)
[37. 44. 30. 26. 29. 27. 32.]
[37. 37. 38. 49.]

Rows, comma, columns: temps[0, :7] is row 0 (Boise) and the first seven columns (days). temps[:, 0] is every row, column 0: one value per city. Boise's week matches the temperatures in the lesson's Exercise 1.

print(temps[2].mean().round(1))     # Flagstaff, average high over two years
print(temps.max(), temps.min())     # hottest and coldest high anywhere
print(np.shares_memory(temps, high))
60.0
112.0 10.0
True

temps[2] is all of Flagstaff's row. The last line prints True: reshaping gave the same memory a new shape, so temps is a view of high, not a copy.

Glossary

Virtual environmentA folder (.venv) holding one project's own copy of Python packages, separate from every other project.
PackageA library someone else wrote that you install and import, such as NumPy or pandas.
requirements.txtA text file listing the packages a project needs, one per line, usually with version pins.
NotebookA file of cells (.ipynb) that you run one at a time, with each result shown under its cell.
KernelThe Python process that runs a notebook's cells. It must be the course environment's Python.
ScriptA .py file that runs from top to bottom, starting fresh every time.
Relative pathA file location given from the folder Python is working in, such as data/orders.csv.
TracebackThe report Python prints when an error stops it. The last line names the error.
DataFramepandas' table: rows and named columns. Taught properly in Module 2.
Array (ndarray)NumPy's container: many values of one type in one block of memory.
dtypeThe single type shared by every element of an array, such as float64 or int64.
shapeThe length of an array along each dimension, as a tuple: (7,) or (3, 4).
VectorizationDoing an operation on a whole array in one expression instead of a Python loop.
ViewAn array that shares memory with another array. Writing to one changes the other.
CopyAn independent array with its own memory, made with .copy().
nan"Not a number": NumPy's marker for a missing value. Arithmetic that touches it gives nan.
Instructor Kit · Participant Workbook

Session 2: Vectorized Operations and Broadcasting

Your working notebook for Session 2. In the first half you stop writing loops over numbers: you describe what you want done to a whole array, and answer two-part questions with masks. In the second half you learn the two rules that let NumPy combine a table with a row or a column, so you can compare each store with its own normal in one line.

Lessons 1.2–1.3 Session 2 · 3 hours Name: ____________________
How to use this workbook

Each lesson has By the end you can, Key ideas, a Worked example (new code with its real output), the Guided lab you do in class, Predict, then run items, the lesson project (started in class, finished at home), a six-question Self-check and the Journal prompt. Lab files are in labs/module-02/: open the _starter.py file and run it once before you change anything. Today's habit: say the shape or the count you expect before you run a line, and check it after.

Session Goals

  • Do arithmetic between arrays that line up by position, and use ufuncs such as np.maximum and np.abs.
  • Build a mask, then count with it, filter with it, and combine two masks with &, | and ~.
  • Choose values by condition with np.where and np.select.
  • Summarize a table along rows or columns and predict the shape of the result.
  • State the two broadcasting rules and predict a result shape, or an error, on paper.
  • Combine a table with a row and with a column (keepdims=True), and build a grid from two flat arrays.
  • Catch the mistakes that raise no error by checking shapes and adding up the pieces.
  • Start both lesson projects: the store scorecard and the weekday index.

Lessons

1.2

Vectorized Operations

By the end you can
  • Combine arrays element by element with arithmetic and with universal functions such as np.sqrt, np.log10 and np.maximum
  • Build boolean masks with comparisons, combine them with &, | and ~, and use them to count, filter and assign
  • Choose values by condition with np.where and np.select
  • Summarize a two-dimensional array along rows or columns with axis=, and predict the shape of the result
  • Rewrite a Python loop over numbers as an array expression and check that both give the same answer
Key ideas
  • Vectorized means one operation on a whole array, with the loop running inside NumPy's compiled code.
  • Parallel arrays line up by position: element 0 of every column describes the same store-day. A mask built from one column can filter another.
  • A comparison makes a mask of True and False. Its sum counts, its mean is a fraction, and inside square brackets it filters.
  • **Combine masks with & (and), | (or), ~ (not)**, and put parentheses around every comparison. Never and / or.
  • **np.where(cond, a, b) is an if/else for every element. np.select** handles three or more outcomes; the first true condition wins.
  • The axis you name is the axis that disappears. For a days-by-stores table, axis=0 gives one value per store and axis=1 one per day. Check the shape of the result.
  • **argmax and argsort return positions.** Use a position to look something up in a parallel array: days[week.argmax()].
  • **nan fails every comparison**, so missing values silently drop out of both groups. Test with np.isnan on purpose.
  • A mask gives a copy; a slice gives a view. Assign through the mask (x[x > 10] = 10) to change the array itself.
You want to...Write
In a loop: for x in values: out.append(x * k)values * k
if cond: count += 1(cond).sum()
if cond: keep.append(x)values[cond]
if x > limit: ... else: ...np.where(values > limit, ..., ...)
if / elif / elsenp.select([...], [...], default=...)
max(0, x) for each xnp.maximum(values, 0)
Both conditions true(a > 1) & (b == 2)
One total per column / per rowt.sum(axis=0) / t.sum(axis=1)
Where is the largest?x.argmax()
Tally the distinct valuesnp.unique(labels, return_counts=True)
Worked example: do sale days mean bigger baskets?

On sale days the stores take in almost twice the revenue. Is that because each customer buys more items, or because there are more customers? units / transactions is the number of items in an average purchase.

units = load(4)
transactions = load(3)
promo = load(7)

per_sale = units / transactions            # items per purchase, every store-day
print(per_sale[:4].round(2))
print(per_sale.mean().round(2), per_sale.min().round(2), per_sale.max().round(2))
[2.16 2.02 2.18 2.09]
2.11 1.68 2.68

One division gives 2,903 answers. A typical purchase is about two items, and the figure never strays far: 1.68 to 2.68.

is_promo = promo == 1

print(is_promo.sum(), "sale store-days")
print(per_sale[is_promo].mean().round(2), "items per purchase on sale days")
print(per_sale[~is_promo].mean().round(2), "items per purchase on ordinary days")
96 sale store-days
2.11 items per purchase on sale days
2.11 items per purchase on ordinary days

The mask is built from promo and used on per_sale: parallel arrays. The two averages are the same to two decimals. Sale days do not make baskets bigger.

print(transactions[is_promo].mean().round(1), "purchases on a sale day")
print(transactions[~is_promo].mean().round(1), "purchases on an ordinary day")

large = per_sale >= 2.3
print(large.sum(), "large-basket days;", (large & is_promo).sum(), "of them were sale days")
155.4 purchases on a sale day
81.5 purchases on an ordinary day
194 large-basket days; 0 of them were sale days

What changes is the number of purchases. And of the store-days with unusually large baskets, none at all were sale days. An honest "no difference" is a finding too: the sale brings more buyers, not bigger baskets.

1.3

Broadcasting

By the end you can
  • State the broadcasting rules and use them to predict the shape of a result, or that an operation will fail
  • Combine a 2-D table with a row of values and with a column of values
  • Reshape an array for broadcasting with np.newaxis, reshape and keepdims=True
  • Build a grid of every combination from two 1-D arrays
  • Recognize the broadcasting mistakes that raise no error, and catch them by checking shapes
Key ideas
  • Broadcasting lets NumPy combine arrays of different shapes by reusing the smaller one, like a rubber stamp pressed on every line of a form.
  • Rule 1: write the shapes one above the other, lined up on the right. A missing entry counts as 1.
  • Rule 2: each pair of lengths must be equal, or one of them is 1. The result takes the larger of each pair. Any pair that fails is an error.
  • Per column (per store): a table and a row. table - table.mean(axis=0) just works.
  • Per row (per day): a table and a column. table / table.sum(axis=1, keepdims=True).
  • A column meeting a row builds a grid of every combination, whether you wanted one or not.
  • Standardizing is one line: (table - table.mean(axis=0)) / table.std(axis=0). It puts columns of different sizes on one scale.
  • The dangerous mistakes are silent. After any operation that mixes shapes, print .shape: four values in should mean four values out.
You want to...Write
Predict a result shape without computingnp.broadcast_shapes((7, 4), (4,))
Each column minus its own meant - t.mean(axis=0)
Each row divided by its own totalt / t.sum(axis=1, keepdims=True)
Flat array to a columna[:, np.newaxis] or a.reshape(-1, 1)
Flat array to a row with two dimensionsa[np.newaxis, :]
Every combination of two flat arraysa[:, np.newaxis] * b
z-scores per column(t - t.mean(axis=0)) / t.std(axis=0)
Any shape back to flata.ravel()
Worked example: how many staff does each store need?

Week 1 of visitors as a 7 by 4 table (days by stores: Asheville, Bend, Boise, Flagstaff). Three questions, three shapes: a table and a row, a table and a column, and a column meeting a row.

visitors = load(2)
V = visitors[:28].reshape(7, 4)

store_mean = V.mean(axis=0)                 # shape (4,)
print(store_mean.round(1))
print((V - store_mean).round(0)[-2:])       # Saturday and Sunday, against each store's own average
[239.3 268.4 380.4 207.4]
[[139. 156. 119.  38.]
 [-28.  16.  84.  48.]]

(7, 4) with (4,): from the right, 4 meets 4, and 7 meets a missing axis. The row of averages is reused on all seven days. Saturday is far above normal at every store.

share = V / V.sum(axis=1, keepdims=True)    # (7, 4) with (7, 1)
print(share.round(2)[:2])                   # Monday and Tuesday
print(share.sum(axis=1))
[[0.23 0.23 0.38 0.16]
 [0.23 0.24 0.33 0.2 ]]
[1. 1. 1. 1. 1. 1. 1.]

(7, 4) with (7, 1): 4 meets 1, 7 meets 7. Each day is divided by its own total, and the check line shows every row adding up to 1. Without keepdims=True the totals would be flat, 4 would meet 7, and NumPy would refuse.

per_staff = np.array([40, 50, 60])          # visitors one staff member can look after
needed = np.ceil(store_mean / per_staff[:, np.newaxis])    # (4,) with (3, 1)
print(needed.shape)
print(needed)
(3, 4)
[[ 6.  7. 10.  6.]
 [ 5.  6.  8.  5.]
 [ 4.  5.  7.  4.]]

(4,) with (3, 1): 4 meets 1, and a missing axis meets 3. A row meets a column, so the result is a 3 by 4 grid: one row per staffing level, one column per store. At 50 visitors per staff member Boise needs 8 people on an average day and Asheville 5.

Glossary

Vectorized operationOne operation applied to a whole array, with the loop running inside NumPy.
Parallel arraysArrays of the same length where position k in each describes the same thing (the same store-day).
ufunc (universal function)A NumPy function that applies itself to every element: np.sqrt, np.abs, np.maximum.
Boolean maskAn array of True and False made by a comparison. Used to count, filter and assign.
**&, |, ~**And, or, not for masks. Each comparison needs its own parentheses.
np.whereA vectorized if/else: np.where(condition, value_if_true, value_if_false).
np.selectA vectorized if/elif/else for three or more outcomes; the first true condition wins.
AggregationAn operation that boils many values down to one: sum, mean, max, std.
axisThe dimension an aggregation collapses. The axis you name is the axis that disappears.
argmax / argsortThe position of the largest value / the positions that would sort the array.
BroadcastingNumPy's rules for combining arrays of different shapes by reusing the smaller one.
keepdims=TrueKeeps the collapsed axis with length 1, so the result is a column (or row) that broadcasts.
np.newaxisAdds an axis of length 1 when indexing: a[:, np.newaxis] turns (n,) into (n, 1).
GridThe table of every combination that results when a column meets a row.
z-scoreHow many standard deviations a value is above or below its column's mean.

Session 3: Randomness and Linear Algebra

Your working notebook for Session 3, the last of the NumPy module. In the first half you make your own numbers: random draws you can reproduce, fair samples of real rows, and simulations that answer "what could happen?". In the second half you meet the working parts of linear algebra, with no proofs: weighted sums, the @ operator, solving for unknowns, and fitting a line through real data.

1.4

Random Number Generation

By the end you can
  • Create a random number generator with np.random.default_rng and draw floats, integers and arrays of any shape from it
  • Explain what a seed does, and structure your code so its random results can be reproduced
  • Sample from an array with and without replacement, shuffle it, and split rows at random
  • Draw from the normal, Poisson and binomial distributions and say what kind of quantity each one models
  • Estimate a probability by simulation, and judge how far to trust the estimate and the model behind it
Key ideas
  • Create one generator, once, with a seed: rng = np.random.default_rng(42). Draw everything from it.
  • A seed fixes where the sequence starts. The generator then moves forward with every draw, like a bookmark. Reproducible means: same code, run from the top, same result.
  • **rng.integers(low, high) leaves out the upper end.** A die is integers(1, 7). size can be a shape.
  • **rng.choice draws from your own values.** With replacement by default; replace=False gives different items; p sets the chances.
  • Sample row positions, then use them on every column, so parallel arrays stay matched.
  • **rng.permutation returns a shuffled copy. rng.shuffle works in place and returns None.**
  • Normal for measurements around a typical value, Poisson for counts of events, binomial for successes out of n tries.
  • Simulation: model, repeat, count. Describe one case with random draws, simulate many at once, take the mean of a mask.
  • More simulations make an estimate more precise. Only a better model makes it more true. Check the model against real data.
You want to...Write
Floats from 0 up to 1rng.random(5)
Ten dicerng.integers(1, 7, size=10)
A table of drawsrng.integers(0, 100, size=(3, 4))
Draw from your own valuesrng.choice(values, size=6)
Different items onlyrng.choice(values, size=3, replace=False)
Five different row positionsrng.choice(n, size=5, replace=False)
A shuffled copy / shuffle in placerng.permutation(x) / rng.shuffle(x)
A random 80/20 splitorder = rng.permutation(n); order[:cut], order[cut:]
Counts of events / successes in n triesrng.poisson(lam, size) / rng.binomial(n, p, size)
A chance, from a simulation(sim > 130).mean()
Worked example: how good is an average from only 40 days?

Suppose Asheville's door counter worked on only 40 days. How far from the truth could a 40-day average of visitors be? You have all 727 real days, so you can find out by sampling.

import numpy as np

def load(col, dtype=float):
    """Read one column of daily_sales.csv into an array."""
    return np.genfromtxt("data/daily_sales.csv", delimiter=",",
                         skip_header=1, usecols=col, dtype=dtype)

stores = load(1, dtype=str)
visitors = load(2)
asheville = visitors[stores == "Asheville"]
print(asheville.size, "days | true average", asheville.mean().round(1))
727 days | true average 274.9

The true average over all 727 days is 274.9 visitors. Normally you would not know it; here it lets you judge the samples.

rng = np.random.default_rng(21)

sample = rng.choice(asheville, size=40, replace=False)     # 40 different days
print(sample[:8])
print("estimate from 40 days:", sample.mean().round(1))
[238. 234. 173. 178. 403. 282. 199. 286.]
estimate from 40 days: 276.0

One sample of 40 different days gives one estimate. It is close, and a different sample would give a different number. How different?

many = rng.choice(asheville, size=(10_000, 40))            # 10,000 samples of 40 days, one per row
estimates = many.mean(axis=1)
low, high = np.percentile(estimates, [5, 95])
print(many.shape, estimates.shape)
print(f"90% of 40-day estimates fall between {low:.0f} and {high:.0f}")
(10000, 40) (10000,)
90% of 40-day estimates fall between 253 and 299

Ten thousand samples at once: size=(10_000, 40) is one sample per row, and mean(axis=1) collapses the 40 days. Nine times in ten, a 40-day average lands between 253 and 299: within about 23 visitors of the truth.

bigger = rng.choice(asheville, size=(10_000, 160)).mean(axis=1)
low, high = np.percentile(bigger, [5, 95])
print(f"90% of 160-day estimates fall between {low:.0f} and {high:.0f}")
print(estimates.std().round(1), bigger.std().round(1))
90% of 160-day estimates fall between 263 and 287
14.1 7.0

With four times as many days the range is half as wide (263 to 287), and the spread of the estimates halves from 14.1 to 7.0. More data helps, with diminishing returns. Module 4 gives this a name and a formula.

np.random.default_rng(2).choice(np.array(["A", "B", "C"]), size=5, replace=False)

1.5

Linear Algebra Essentials

By the end you can
  • Compute a dot product and explain it as a weighted sum
  • Multiply matrices and vectors with @, predict the shape of the result, and tell @ apart from *
  • Solve a system of linear equations with np.linalg.solve and check the answer
  • Fit a line or a multi-feature linear model with np.linalg.lstsq, and interpret its coefficients and its error
  • Measure length and distance with np.linalg.norm, and standardize features before comparing them
Key ideas
  • A dot product multiplies matching positions and adds them up: a weighted sum. a @ b. Two vectors in, one number out.
  • matrix @ vector takes the dot product of every row with the vector: many totals, or many predictions, at once.
  • The shape rule: (n, k) @ (k, m) gives (n, m). The inner numbers must match, and order matters.
  • *** is position by position and keeps the shape. @ multiplies and adds.** When shapes broadcast, * gives the wrong thing with no error: check the shape.
  • **.T** swaps rows and columns.
  • **np.linalg.solve(A, b)** finds the unknowns of an exact system. Check with np.allclose(A @ x, b).
  • **np.linalg.lstsq(A, y)** finds the best-fitting weights when there are more equations than unknowns. That is linear regression. A column of ones gives the intercept; more features are more columns.
  • RMSE is the typical miss, in the units of what you predict. R squared is the share of the variation the model accounts for.
  • A fitted line describes; it does not prove. Read a coefficient as "goes with", with the other columns held fixed.
  • **np.linalg.norm** measures length; the norm of a difference is a distance. Standardize the columns first.
You want to...Write
A weighted sumquantities @ prices
A weighted averageratings @ reviews / reviews.sum()
One total (or prediction) per rowtable @ weights
Rows become columnsM.T
Solve an exact systemnp.linalg.solve(A, b)
Build the matrix for a lineA = np.column_stack([np.ones(x.size), x])
Fit by least squarescoef, *_ = np.linalg.lstsq(A, y)
Predictions and residualsA @ coef and y - A @ coef
Typical miss (RMSE)np.sqrt((residuals ** 2).mean())
Distance from row 0 to every rownp.linalg.norm(z - z[0], axis=1)
Worked example: which product is most like this one?

A shopper is looking at a pair of trail runners. Which other footwear product is most like it, judged by price and weight? products.csv has both columns (5 is price, 7 is weight in kilograms; 2 is the category and 3 the product type).

import numpy as np

def col(k, dtype=float):
    return np.genfromtxt("data/products.csv", delimiter=",", skip_header=1, usecols=k, dtype=dtype)

category, kind = col(2, dtype=str), col(3, dtype=str)
price, weight = col(5), col(7)

keep = (category == "Footwear") & ~np.isnan(weight)
shoes = np.column_stack([price[keep], weight[keep]])     # one row per product: price, weight_kg
kinds = kind[keep]
print(shoes.shape)
print(kinds[1], shoes[1])
(26, 2)
Trail Runners [135.95   0.57]

One mask (footwear, and a weight present) applied to every column keeps them parallel. np.column_stack puts price and weight side by side: 26 products, 2 features. Row 1 is a pair of trail runners at $135.95 and 0.57 kg.

raw = np.linalg.norm(shoes - shoes[1], axis=1)           # a table minus a row, then one norm per row
raw[1] = np.inf                                          # a product is not its own neighbor
print("closest on raw numbers:", kinds[raw.argmin()], shoes[raw.argmin()])
closest on raw numbers: Hiking Boots [135.95   1.21]

shoes - shoes[1] is a table minus a row, and axis=1 asks for one norm per row. The "closest" product is a pair of hiking boots: exactly the same price, and more than twice the weight. Price is measured in tens and hundreds, weight in fractions of a kilogram, so price decided everything.

z = (shoes - shoes.mean(axis=0)) / shoes.std(axis=0)     # every column on one scale
dist = np.linalg.norm(z - z[1], axis=1)
dist[1] = np.inf
print("closest after standardizing:", kinds[dist.argmin()], shoes[dist.argmin()])
closest after standardizing: Trail Runners [132.95   0.62]

After standardizing (the one-line step from Lesson 1.3), both columns pull equal weight, and the nearest product is another pair of trail runners: $3 cheaper and 50 grams heavier. That is what a shopper would call similar.

Glossary

**Generator (rng)**The object that produces random numbers: rng = np.random.default_rng(seed).
SeedA whole number that fixes where the generator's sequence starts, so results can be reproduced.
Pseudo-randomProduced by a fixed recipe, yet statistically indistinguishable from random for sampling and simulation.
With / without replacementWhether a drawn value can be drawn again. replace=False gives different items.
Sampling variationThe gap between what the probabilities say and what one particular sample shows.
DistributionA description of which values are likely: normal, Poisson, binomial, and many more.
Monte Carlo simulationAnswering a question by modeling one case with random draws, repeating it many times, and counting.
Law of large numbersThe average of more and more random draws settles toward the true underlying value.
Vector / matrixA 1-D array / a 2-D array.
Dot productMultiply matching positions and add them up: a weighted sum. Written a @ b.
**Transpose (.T)**The same matrix with rows and columns swapped.
Least squaresChoosing the weights that make the sum of squared misses as small as possible. Linear regression.
CoefficientA fitted weight: how much the prediction changes per unit of one feature, with the others held fixed.
ResidualAn actual value minus the model's prediction for it.
RMSERoot mean squared error: the typical size of a miss, in the units of what is predicted.
R squaredThe share of the variation that a model accounts for: 1 is a perfect fit, 0 no better than the mean.
Norm / distanceThe length of a vector / the norm of the difference between two vectors.

Lessons

2.1

DataFrames and Series

90 min lesson
By the end you can
  • Describe a Series as a labeled array and a DataFrame as a set of Series that share one index
  • Inspect any table with shape, head(), info(), dtypes and describe(), and read the dtypes pandas reports
  • Select columns with [], rows and cells by label with .loc and by position with .iloc, and filter rows with boolean masks
  • Add and change columns in a way that works under copy-on-write, and explain why chained assignment never does
  • Sort a table and count the values in a column with sort_values() and value_counts()
Key ideas
  • The labeled shelf. A Series is an array of values plus an index of labels (plus a name). A DataFrame is several Series side by side that share one index. Each column keeps its own dtype.
  • Alignment. Arithmetic between two Series matches labels, not positions. A label with no partner gives NaN. Unexpected NaNs? Compare the two indexes.
  • The four-line greeting. df.shape, df.head(), df.info(), df.describe() on every new table before anything else. In info(), a Non-Null Count below the row count means missing values.
  • dtypes. int64 whole numbers, float64 decimals (and any numeric column with gaps), str text, bool, datetime64[us] dates. A date read from a file is str until you convert it. object on a column today usually means mixed types: investigate.
  • Three selection tools. df["col"] is a Series; df[["a", "b"]] is a DataFrame. .loc[rows, cols] uses labels and its slices include the end. .iloc[rows, cols] uses positions and its slices exclude the stop, like NumPy.
  • Masks. A comparison makes a True/False Series; df[mask] keeps the True rows. Combine with &, |, ~ and put every condition in its own parentheses. .sum() of a mask counts, .mean() gives the share. Helpers: .isin([...]), .between(a, b), .str.contains(...), .isna().
  • Changing a table. New column: df["new"] = ... (whole-column arithmetic, no loop). Some rows: df.loc[mask, "col"] = value. If/else: np.where(cond, a, b).
  • Copy-on-write. Every piece you take from a table behaves as an independent copy. So df[mask]["col"] = value (chained assignment) never changes df. It only warns.
  • Sort and count. sort_values("col", ascending=False) returns a new table and the row labels travel with their rows. value_counts() counts categories (missing values left out unless dropna=False); normalize=True gives shares.
Worked example: how rainy is each store's city?

A table you have not been walked through: store_weather.csv, one row per city per day. The question is small on purpose, so you can watch each move: greet, add a column from a comparison, use a mask for the rows and a name for the column, find a row by its label.

Greet the table. Four cities times 731 days is 2,924 rows; the date is text for now.

weather = pd.read_csv("data/store_weather.csv")
print(weather.shape)
print(weather.head(3))
print(weather.dtypes)
(2924, 5)
         date   city  high_f  low_f  precip_in
0  2024-01-01  Boise      37     24        0.0
1  2024-01-02  Boise      44     32        0.0
2  2024-01-03  Boise      30     14        0.0
date             str
city             str
high_f         int64
low_f          int64
precip_in    float64
dtype: object

A comparison is a True/False column. Store it, then count it (sum) and take its share (mean).

weather["rainy"] = weather["precip_in"] >= 0.10
print(weather["rainy"].sum(), round(weather["rainy"].mean() * 100, 1))
389 13.3

.loc[mask, "rainy"] means: these rows, that column. One mask per city.

for city in ["Boise", "Bend", "Flagstaff", "Asheville"]:
    in_city = weather["city"] == city
    print(f"{city:<10}{weather.loc[in_city, 'rainy'].mean() * 100:5.1f}% rainy days")
Boise       9.3% rainy days
Bend        6.8% rainy days
Flagstaff  13.5% rainy days
Asheville  23.5% rainy days

Where is the hottest day? idxmax() gives its row label, and .loc reads that row. Then a new column from two others, and the three widest daily swings.

hot = weather["high_f"].idxmax()
print(hot, weather.loc[hot, "date"], weather.loc[hot, "city"], weather.loc[hot, "high_f"])
weather["swing_f"] = weather["high_f"] - weather["low_f"]
print(weather.nlargest(3, "swing_f")[["date", "city", "high_f", "low_f", "swing_f"]])
559 2025-07-13 Boise 112
            date  city  high_f  low_f  swing_f
1338  2025-08-30  Bend      74     38       36
1233  2025-05-17  Bend      59     24       35
1300  2025-07-23  Bend      93     58       35

Asheville has rain on about one day in four; Bend on about one day in fifteen. You answered that with a new column, a mask and .loc, and no loop over rows (the short for loop runs four times, once per city, not once per row). In Lesson 2.4 groupby will replace even that loop.

2.2

Loading Data

85 min lesson
By the end you can
  • Read a CSV file with the options that matter: columns, dtypes, dates, missing-value markers, separators and header position
  • Read a sheet from an Excel workbook whose header is not on the first row
  • Flatten nested JSON into tables with pd.json_normalize()
  • Process a file in chunks and shrink its memory use with usecols and dtype
  • Write a DataFrame to CSV, Excel, JSON and Parquet, and explain what each format keeps and loses
Key ideas
  • Loading is translation. A text file carries no types. The reader guesses; you brief it with options and then check the result: shape, dtypes, head() after every load.
  • read_csv options that earn their keep. parse_dates=[...] real dates · usecols=[...] only these columns · dtype={...} set a type · index_col · nrows=5 a peek · na_values=[...] extra words for missing · sep=";" · skiprows / header · decimal, thousands · encoding.
  • Three types to set yourself. Dates (they stay str unless parsed). "Int64" (capital I) for whole numbers with gaps: ids, years, counts. "category" for text with few distinct values. Codes with leading zeros (ZIP codes) are text: dtype={"zip": "str"}.
  • Excel. pd.ExcelFile(path).sheet_names lists the sheets. Unnamed columns mean the header is not where pandas looked: skiprows=3 or header=3. To see the top of a sheet as it is: header=None, nrows=8.
  • Nested JSON. json.load the file, find the list of records, pd.json_normalize(records). Inner dictionaries become dotted columns (device.type). A missing key becomes NaN. For a list inside each record: record_path="pages", meta=["session_id"] gives one row per item.
  • Big files. Peek (nrows), load fewer columns (usecols), use smaller dtypes, then chunksize and a running total. Sums and counts combine across chunks; a mean needs a sum and a count; a median cannot be done this way.
  • Writing. Every reader has a writer. to_csv(path, index=False) for people and other tools: it forgets every dtype. to_parquet(path) for your future self: smaller, and dates, categories and Int64 come back as they were.
Worked example: a typed load of the checkout experiment

ab_test.csv has 28,000 rows, one per visitor in a checkout-page experiment (you will analyze it in Module 4). Here the only job is to get it in with the right types and see what that buys you. Follow the order: peek, load plainly, look, brief the reader, look again, save.

Peek at three rows. You learn the column names and can plan the real load.

print(pd.read_csv("data/ab_test.csv", nrows=3))
  visitor_id        date  variant   device  returning  converted  order_value
0    V024143  2025-09-08  control  desktop      False          0          NaN
1    V012790  2025-09-08  control  desktop      False          0          NaN
2    V004888  2025-09-08  control  desktop       True          0          NaN

Load it plainly and measure. Three text columns, and the date is one of them.

plain = pd.read_csv("data/ab_test.csv")
print(plain.dtypes)
print(round(plain.memory_usage(deep=True).sum() / 1024**2, 2), "MB")
visitor_id         str
date               str
variant            str
device             str
returning         bool
converted        int64
order_value    float64
dtype: object
2.15 MB

Brief the reader: the date is a date, and two columns hold only a few distinct values.

ab = pd.read_csv("data/ab_test.csv", parse_dates=["date"],
                 dtype={"variant": "category", "device": "category"})
print(ab.dtypes)
print(round(ab.memory_usage(deep=True).sum() / 1024**2, 2), "MB")
print(ab["date"].min().date(), "to", ab["date"].max().date())
visitor_id                str
date           datetime64[us]
variant              category
device               category
returning                bool
converted               int64
order_value           float64
dtype: object
1.12 MB
2025-09-08 to 2025-10-05

Check the translation with numbers you can reason about, then save a copy that remembers its types.

print(ab["converted"].sum(), ab["order_value"].notna().sum(), ab["order_value"].isna().sum())
from pathlib import Path
Path("output").mkdir(exist_ok=True)
ab.to_parquet("output/ab_test.parquet")
print(pd.read_parquet("output/ab_test.parquet").equals(ab))
2645 2645 25355
True

The typed table uses far less memory than the plain one, the date has a real minimum and maximum, and the two counts agree: 2,645 visitors converted and exactly 2,645 rows have an order value. The other 25,355 order values are missing because those visitors bought nothing: missing here means 'not applicable', not 'lost'.

Glossary for Session 4

  • Series: a one-dimensional labeled array: values, an index and an optional name.
  • DataFrame: a table: several Series side by side that share one index.
  • Index: the row labels of a Series or DataFrame. The default is RangeIndex: 0, 1, 2, ...
  • dtype: the type of every value in a column: int64, float64, str, bool, datetime64[us], category, Int64.
  • NaN / <NA>: pandas's markers for a missing value. Test for them with .isna(), never with ==.
  • Alignment: matching by label, not by position, when two Series or tables are combined.
  • Boolean mask: a Series of True/False values used to keep the rows where it is True.
  • .loc / .iloc: selection by label (slices include the end) / by integer position (slices exclude the stop).
  • Copy-on-write: the rule that every object derived from another behaves as an independent copy.
  • Chained assignment: df[mask]["col"] = value: two selections and then an assignment. It never changes df.
  • Nullable integer (Int64): a whole-number dtype that can also hold a missing value.
  • Category: a dtype that stores each distinct value once plus a small code per row; good for repetitive text.
  • json_normalize: the pandas function that flattens nested dictionaries into dotted columns; record_path gives one row per item of an inner list.
  • Chunk: one piece of a file read with chunksize, delivered as an ordinary DataFrame.
  • Parquet: a binary, column-by-column file format that stores each column's dtype with the data.

Session 5: Cleaning and Grouping

Your working pages for two of the most used skills in data work. Before the break: turning a damaged file into a table you can trust, and proving that you did. After the break: answering every question that starts with 'for each...'.

Lessons 2.3–2.4 Session 5 · 3 hours Name: ____________________
How to use this workbook

Each lesson has By the end you can, Key ideas (one page to keep), a Worked example with its real output, the Guided lab you do in class, three Predict, then run boxes, the lesson project's class and home parts, space for notes, and a six-question Self-check. Lab files are in labs/module-05/. Two habits for today: count the rows before and after every step, and, after the break, ask what shape you want back before you type a groupby.

Session Goals

  • Audit a table you have never seen and write a repair list before changing anything
  • Repair structure, text, numbers and dates in code, on a copy, checking each step
  • Decide what to do with each kind of missing or suspicious value, and say why
  • Summarize 'for each...' questions with groupby and named aggregation
  • Choose between agg, transform and filter by the shape of the answer
  • Catch the quiet traps: lost rows, count versus size, averages of averages

Lessons

2.3

Cleaning Data

100 min lesson
By the end you can
  • Audit an unfamiliar table and list its problems before changing anything
  • Repair structure: untidy headers, summary rows and duplicate rows
  • Standardize inconsistent text and convert numbers and dates that were stored as text, including dates in several formats
  • Decide, and defend, what to do with each kind of missing or suspicious value: repair, fill, flag or leave
  • Verify a cleaning job with a before-and-after audit and assertions, and package it as a function you can rerun
Key ideas
  • The repair bench. Audit first and write the repair list. Fix one thing at a time, in code, on a copy. Check each repair. The raw file is read-only.
  • The order. Structure (headers, rows that are not data, duplicates) → text → numbers → dates → missing values. Fixing the shape first makes every later step simpler.
  • Audit by counting. df.dtypes, df.isna().sum(), df.nunique(), df.duplicated().sum(). Print df.columns.tolist() to see hidden spaces. A category column with more distinct values than the business has is damaged.
  • Text. .str.strip(), then .str.title() / .str.lower() / .str.upper(), then a mapping dictionary with .replace({...}), built from value_counts() after the mechanical steps. Placeholders such as none and - become real missing values with .where(...).
  • Numbers. pd.to_numeric(s, errors="coerce") is a detector: what became NaN but was not empty? Remove symbols with .str.replace("$", ""), then convert without errors= as a guarantee. Then .describe(): is the minimum and maximum plausible?
  • An outlier is a question. Never change a value for being extreme. Look for independent evidence (here: the order's item lines), then repair only the rows the evidence points to, with one .loc.
  • Dates. One pd.to_datetime(d, format=..., errors="coerce") per known format, combined with .fillna(). Impossible dates become NaT and stay missing.
  • Missing values are decisions. It is not missing (no promo code) · repair (a rule or another source) · leave and report · drop for one analysis with dropna(subset=[...]). Filling a number invents data. Write the decision down.
  • Prove it. Run the audit again and compare. State the rules as assert lines. Package the job as a function so it can be rerun.
Worked example: what is missing in the survey, and what should we do about it?

survey.csv is tidy: no bad headers, no duplicates. Its problem is the quiet one, missing answers. This example practices the part of cleaning that is judgment, not typing: look at what is missing, find out why, and decide.

Audit first. Seven columns have gaps, and two of them have hundreds.

survey = pd.read_csv("data/survey.csv")
print(len(survey), survey.duplicated(subset=["customer_id"]).sum())
missing = survey.isna().sum()
print(missing[missing > 0])
1800 0
sat_overall       53
sat_selection     53
sat_price         47
sat_staff        605
sat_website      472
recommend         54
comment          977
dtype: int64

What would dropna() do? Print the row count before and after every drop.

print(len(survey), "->", len(survey.dropna()))
print(len(survey), "->", len(survey.dropna(subset=["sat_overall", "recommend"])))
1800 -> 295
1800 -> 1699

Why are 605 staff ratings missing? Compare the share of missing answers for online shoppers and store shoppers (the mean of a True/False column is a share).

online = survey["store"] == "Online"
for label, rows in [("online shoppers", online), ("store shoppers ", ~online)]:
    print(label, round(survey.loc[rows, "sat_staff"].isna().mean() * 100, 1), "% missing staff rating,",
          round(survey.loc[rows, "sat_website"].isna().mean() * 100, 1), "% missing website rating")
online shoppers 69.9 % missing staff rating, 2.9 % missing website rating
store shoppers  3.0 % missing staff rating, 45.9 % missing website rating

What would filling claim? Compare the mean as it is with the mean after filling every gap with a neutral 3.

print(survey["sat_staff"].mean().round(2), survey["sat_staff"].fillna(3).mean().round(2))
assert survey["sat_staff"].dropna().between(1, 5).all()
assert survey["recommend"].dropna().between(0, 10).all()
print("all checks passed")
4.02 3.68
all checks passed

Decision: leave the gaps and report them. Seven in ten online shoppers skipped the staff question because they never met staff: for them the honest value is 'does not apply', and filling it with 3 would pull the staff score from 4.02 down to 3.68 with invented answers. Dropping every row with any gap would keep 295 of 1,800 responses. What you write in the report: "Staff satisfaction is based on the 1,195 customers who answered, almost all of them store shoppers."

2.4

GroupBy Operations

90 min lesson
By the end you can
  • Explain split-apply-combine and read a groupby expression piece by piece
  • Summarize groups with built-in methods and with named aggregation, on one key or several
  • Choose correctly between agg (one row per group), transform (one row per original row) and filter (keep or drop whole groups)
  • Control what happens to missing keys and unused categories with dropna= and observed=
  • Recognize the common traps: lost rows, a key hidden in the index, and averages of averages
Key ideas
  • Split, apply, combine. df.groupby("key")["column"].function(): deal the rows into piles by key, compute on each pile, collect one answer per pile. Any question that starts 'for each...' is a groupby.
  • What comes back. A Series or DataFrame whose index is the group key, sorted. Get the key back as a column with .reset_index() or as_index=False.
  • Counting. .size() rows per group · .count() non-missing values of a column · .nunique() distinct values. They answer different questions.
  • Named aggregation. df.groupby("store").agg(orders=("order_id", "count"), revenue=("order_total", "sum")). New column name = (source column, function). Learn this form by heart.
  • Rates. Make a True/False helper column; its mean within each group is the rate.
  • Several keys. A list of keys gives one group per combination and a two-level index (MultiIndex). Flatten with .reset_index() or spread one level into columns with .unstack(). A key can be computed on the spot: orders["order_date"].dt.year.
  • What shape do I want back? One row per group: agg. The same rows with a group number on each: transform. Fewer of the same rows: filter (or a transform and a mask, which is faster).
  • Quiet traps. Rows whose key is missing vanish unless dropna=False: check that the groups add up to len(df). Never average group means of unequal groups. observed=False shows categories that have no rows.
Worked example: what do different age groups think of our prices?

survey.csv again, with a 'for each' question: for each age group, how satisfied are customers with prices and with the website (1 = very dissatisfied, 5 = very satisfied)? The example uses the four moves you will repeat in the lab: named aggregation, a check that the groups add up, transform, and filter.

One row per age group. count on response_id counts responses; count on sat_price counts answers, because count skips missing values.

by_age = survey.groupby("age_group").agg(
    responses=("response_id", "count"),
    answered_price=("sat_price", "count"),
    price=("sat_price", "mean"),
    website=("sat_website", "mean"),
)
print(by_age.round(2))
           responses  answered_price  price  website
age_group                                           
18-24            192             186   2.80     3.69
25-34            280             273   3.32     3.67
35-44            318             309   3.38     3.67
45-54            339             332   3.38     3.66
55-64            279             273   3.44     3.72
65+              392             380   3.33     3.04

Do the groups add up? And is the average of the six group averages the overall average?

print(by_age["responses"].sum(), len(survey))
print(by_age["price"].mean().round(3), survey["sat_price"].mean().round(3))
1800 1800
3.276 3.309

transform gives one value per original row: each response next to its own age group's average.

survey["age_avg_price"] = survey.groupby("age_group")["sat_price"].transform("mean")
print(survey["age_avg_price"].shape)
print(survey[["response_id", "age_group", "sat_price", "age_avg_price"]].head(3).round(2))
(1800,)
   response_id age_group  sat_price  age_avg_price
0            1       65+        5.0           3.33
1            2     18-24        3.0           2.80
2            3     25-34        4.0           3.32

filter keeps whole groups: here, only the responses from stores with at least 250 responses.

busy = survey.groupby("store").filter(lambda g: len(g) >= 250)
print(len(busy), sorted(busy["store"].unique()))
1153 ['Boise', 'Online']

Two groups stand out. Customers aged 18 to 24 rate prices at 2.80 when every other group is between 3.32 and 3.44, and customers aged 65 and over rate the website at 3.04 when every other group is near 3.7. Those are leads to investigate, not conclusions: Module 4 tests whether gaps like these could be chance. The average of the six group averages (3.276) is not the overall average (3.309), because the groups are different sizes.

Predict, then run

Write what you think each one prints before you run it. Being wrong here is useful: it shows you exactly which rule to look at again.

P1. An average of averages.

df = pd.DataFrame({"team": ["a", "b", "a", "b", "a"], "pts": [2, 10, 4, 20, 6]})
means = df.groupby("team")["pts"].mean()
print(means.tolist(), means.mean(), df["pts"].mean())

P2. A key with gaps.

df = pd.DataFrame({"code": ["X", None, "X", None], "amt": [1, 2, 3, 4]})
print(len(df.groupby("code")), len(df.groupby("code", dropna=False)),
      df.groupby("code")["amt"].sum().sum())

P3. Three shapes.

df = pd.DataFrame({"k": ["a", "a", "b"], "v": [1, 2, 3]})
a = df.groupby("k")["v"].sum()
t = df.groupby("k")["v"].transform("sum")
f = df.groupby("k").filter(lambda g: len(g) > 1)
print(len(a), len(t), len(f))

Glossary for Session 5

  • Audit: a systematic count of what is in a table (dtypes, missing values, distinct values, duplicates) before changing anything.
  • Raw file: the data as received. Read-only: all fixes are code applied to a copy.
  • Duplicate: a row that is an exact copy of an earlier row (duplicated(), drop_duplicates()); subset= compares only some columns.
  • Placeholder: text that stands for 'nothing', such as none, - or pending. It must become a real missing value.
  • Coerce: errors="coerce": convert what can be converted and turn the rest into NaN or NaT instead of raising an error.
  • NaT: 'not a time': the missing value of a datetime column.
  • Mapping dictionary: a dictionary of wrong spelling to right spelling, applied with .replace(); it documents each judgment call.
  • Outlier: an extreme value. A question to investigate with independent evidence, not an error by itself.
  • Assertion: an assert line that states a rule and stops the program when the rule is broken.
  • Pipeline: cleaning steps packaged as functions and chained (.pipe()), so the whole job can be rerun.
  • Split-apply-combine: the groupby pattern: split rows into groups by a key, compute on each group, combine the answers.
  • Aggregation: reducing each group to one row (sum, mean, agg).
  • Named aggregation: agg(new_name=("column", "function")): one output column per argument.
  • Transform: a group calculation that returns one value per original row.
  • Filter (groupby): keeping or dropping whole groups according to a test on the group.
  • MultiIndex: an index with more than one level, as produced by grouping on several keys.
  • Average of averages: the mean of group means; it equals the overall mean only when the groups are the same size.

Session 6: Merging and Reshaping

Your working pages for connecting tables and changing their shape. Before the break: merge, the four kinds of join, and the two silent ways a merge goes wrong. After the break: pivot tables, crosstabs and melt, and the checks to run before you trust a grid.

How to use this workbook

Each lesson has By the end you can, Key ideas (one page to keep), a Worked example with its real output, the Guided lab you do in class, three Predict, then run boxes, the lesson project's class and home parts, space for notes, and a six-question Self-check. Lab files are in labs/module-06/. Two habits for today: count before and after every merge, and check that the corner of a pivot matches the source.

Session Goals

  • Combine two tables on a key and choose the right kind of join
  • Predict the row count of a merge, check it, and find the keys that did not match
  • State the relationship between two tables and enforce it with validate=
  • Avoid double counting after a one-to-many merge
  • Build grids with pivot_table and crosstab, with totals and percentages
  • Move between wide and tidy layouts with melt, and check a pivot before you trust it

Lessons

2.5

Merging and Joining

95 min lesson
By the end you can
  • Combine two tables on a key with merge() and choose between inner, left, right and outer joins
  • Find the keys that have no match on either side with indicator=True, and decide what to do about them
  • State the relationship between two tables (one-to-one, many-to-one, many-to-many) and enforce it with validate=
  • Predict and check the row count of a merge, and avoid double counting after a one-to-many merge
  • Merge on differently named or multiple keys, stack tables with concat(), and recognize when merge_asof() is the right tool
Key ideas
  • The claim ticket. A key is a column that appears in both tables. left.merge(right, on="key") finds, for each left row, the right rows with the same key. Two questions: what if a key has no match? What if it matches more than once?
  • Four joins. how="inner" (the default) keeps only keys in both tables · "left" keeps every row of the left table · "right" every row of the right · "outer" every row of both. Missing partners become NaN.
  • Count before, count after. Say what the row count should be, then check. Fewer rows: keys failed to match. More rows: keys matched more than once.
  • The working default. Adding columns to a main table? how="left". Select only the columns you need from the right table (key included) before merging.
  • indicator=True adds _merge: both, left_only, right_only. Unmatched rows are findings with causes (closed accounts, discontinued products). Decide what to do with them; do not let the default decide.
  • Relationships. one-to-one, many-to-one (safe: the row count cannot grow), one-to-many (rows multiply: one per match), many-to-many (usually a mistake). validate="many_to_one" makes pandas check that the key is unique where you believe it is.
  • Know what one row means. After a one-to-many merge, columns from the 'one' side are repeated. Never sum or average them there. Aggregate first, then merge.
  • Awkward keys. Different names: left_on=, right_on=. Several columns: lists. Clashing column names: suffixes=. Keys must have the same dtype. Missing keys match each other.
  • Other tools. pd.concat([a, b], ignore_index=True) stacks tables with the same columns. pd.merge_asof matches the nearest earlier key (both tables sorted).
Worked example: which products never sold, and which sales have no product?

A question that needs two tables: the catalog (products.csv, one row per product) and the order lines (order_items.csv, one row per line). The pattern is the one you will use in the lab: aggregate the long table to one row per key, then merge, and audit both sides.

Aggregate first: one row per SKU that was ever sold. Then check the keys before merging anything.

items["line_total"] = items["quantity"] * items["unit_price"]
sold = items.groupby("sku", as_index=False).agg(units=("quantity", "sum"), revenue=("line_total", "sum"))
print(len(products), len(sold), products["sku"].is_unique, sold["sku"].is_unique)
242 240 True True

Prediction: catalog on the left, one-to-one, 242 rows. A left join keeps every product.

catalog = products[["sku", "name", "category", "launch_date"]].merge(
    sold, on="sku", how="left", validate="one_to_one", indicator=True)
print(len(catalog), catalog["_merge"].value_counts().to_dict())
print(catalog.loc[catalog["_merge"] == "left_only", ["sku", "name", "launch_date"]].head(3))
242 {'both': 234, 'left_only': 8, 'right_only': 0}
         sku                               name launch_date
49  PCK-1002    Pinecrest Headwater 30L Daypack  2025-12-20
56  PCK-1009  Juniper Vane Sawtooth 30L Daypack  2025-12-28
89  FTW-1018     Granite Fork Wayfinder Insoles  2025-12-28

Now audit the other side with an outer join: are there sales with no product in the catalog?

audit = products[["sku"]].merge(sold, on="sku", how="outer", indicator=True)
print(audit["_merge"].value_counts().to_dict())
gone = audit[audit["_merge"] == "right_only"]
print(gone["sku"].tolist(), int(gone["units"].sum()), round(gone["revenue"].sum(), 2))
{'both': 234, 'left_only': 8, 'right_only': 6}
['APL-1033', 'KIT-1016', 'PCK-1015', 'PCK-1025', 'SNW-1007', 'SNW-1018'] 1193 36495.55

With names attached, the best sellers are readable.

print(catalog.nlargest(3, "revenue")[["sku", "name", "units", "revenue"]])
          sku                                name   units    revenue
224  ACC-1015  Cedar Notch Sawtooth First-Aid Kit  3475.0  159029.25
100  APL-1001    Granite Fork Cascade Rain Jacket   420.0  117579.00
48   PCK-1001        Wrenfield Canyon 22L Daypack   784.0  107368.80

Eight products have never sold: all of them launched on or after 2025-12-20, at the very end of the data, so that is no surprise. Six SKUs have sales but no catalog entry: discontinued products, last sold in 2024. Both groups of unmatched keys have a cause, and neither is an error. A default inner join would have hidden both without a word.

Predict, then run

Write what you think each one prints before you run it. Being wrong here is useful: it shows you exactly which rule to look at again.

P1. An outer join with an indicator.

left = pd.DataFrame({"k": [1, 2, 2], "a": ["x", "y", "z"]})
right = pd.DataFrame({"k": [2, 2, 3], "b": [10, 20, 30]})
m = left.merge(right, on="k", how="outer", indicator=True)
print(len(m), (m["_merge"] == "both").sum(), (m["_merge"] == "left_only").sum())

P2. Missing keys.

a = pd.DataFrame({"k": [1.0, np.nan], "a": ["x", "y"]})
b = pd.DataFrame({"k": [np.nan, np.nan], "b": ["p", "q"]})
print(len(a.merge(b, on="k")), len(a.merge(b, on="k", how="left")))

P3. An average after a merge.

o = pd.DataFrame({"order": [1, 2], "total": [300, 100]})
i = pd.DataFrame({"order": [1, 1, 1, 2], "qty": [1, 1, 1, 1]})
print(o["total"].mean(), o.merge(i, on="order")["total"].mean())

2.6

Pivot Tables and Reshaping

85 min lesson
By the end you can
  • Build a summary grid with pivot_table() by choosing its rows, columns, values and aggregation, and add totals with margins=True
  • Count and compare categories with pd.crosstab(), including row and column percentages
  • Choose between pivot() and pivot_table(), and explain the "duplicate entries" error
  • Recognize wide and tidy (long) layouts and convert between them with melt(), stack() and unstack()
  • Check a pivot before you trust it: its aggregation, its cell counts, its empty cells and its totals
Key ideas
  • Logbook and scoreboard. Long (tidy) data has one row per observation: it is for analysis. Wide data spreads one variable across the header: it is for reading. Tidy means each variable is a column, each observation a row, each cell one value.
  • Four questions. df.pivot_table(index=..., columns=..., values=..., aggfunc=...): what is each row, what is each column, which number goes in the cells, how are many rows combined? A pivot table is a groupby spread into a grid.
  • Always write aggfunc=. The default is the mean, not the sum.
  • Totals and empty cells. margins=True adds totals computed from all the underlying rows. A combination that never occurs is NaN: fill_value=0 is right for counts and sums, wrong for averages. The corner of a count pivot should equal the number of source rows.
  • pd.crosstab(a, b) counts combinations. normalize="index": each row sums to 1 ('of online orders, what share were returned?'). normalize="columns": each column sums to 1 ('of returned orders, what share were online?'). Different questions.
  • pivot() versus pivot_table(). pivot() only rearranges and fails with 'duplicate entries' when a cell would get several rows. That error is information about your data.
  • melt(id_vars=..., value_vars=..., var_name=..., value_name=...) turns header labels back into a column. stack() and unstack() do the same moves on index levels.
  • Labels are sorted. Month and weekday names come out alphabetical. Pivot on numbers, or reindex in the order you want.
  • Before you trust a pivot. What is one row of the source? What is the aggregation? How many rows are behind each cell? What do empty cells mean? Do the totals reconcile? Does a total hide a difference inside it?
Worked example: on which days do people order, online and in store?

A counting question with a trap in it. The orders are a logbook; the answer is a small grid: weekdays down the side, the two channels across the top. Watch the label order, and watch which direction the percentages run.

Count the combinations with crosstab. The rows come out in alphabetical order: Friday first.

day = orders["order_date"].dt.day_name().rename("day")
counts = pd.crosstab(day, orders["channel"], margins=True)
print(counts)
channel    online  store    All
day                            
Friday       1677   1098   2775
Monday       2023    874   2897
Saturday     1789   1487   3276
Sunday       2004   1332   3336
Thursday     1727    913   2640
Tuesday      1853    859   2712
Wednesday    1781    885   2666
All         12854   7448  20302

Put the days in calendar order with reindex, and check that the corner is the number of orders.

week = ["Monday", "Tuesday", "Wednesday", "Thursday", "Friday", "Saturday", "Sunday"]
counts = counts.reindex(week + ["All"])
print(counts.index.tolist())
print(counts.loc["All", "All"], len(orders))
['Monday', 'Tuesday', 'Wednesday', 'Thursday', 'Friday', 'Saturday', 'Sunday', 'All']
20302 20302

Raw counts mislead, because there are more online orders on every day. Make each column add up to 100%: of each channel's orders, what share falls on each day?

share = pd.crosstab(day, orders["channel"], normalize="columns").reindex(week) * 100
print(share.round(1))
channel    online  store
day                     
Monday       15.7   11.7
Tuesday      14.4   11.5
Wednesday    13.9   11.9
Thursday     13.4   12.3
Friday       13.0   14.7
Saturday     13.9   20.0
Sunday       15.6   17.9

The grid is for people. Melt it back to a logbook for a program (a chart in Module 3 would want this shape).

tidy = share.round(1).reset_index().melt(id_vars="day", var_name="channel", value_name="pct")
print(tidy.shape)
print(tidy.sort_values("pct", ascending=False).head(3))
(14, 3)
         day channel   pct
12  Saturday   store  20.0
13    Sunday   store  17.9
0     Monday  online  15.7

Stores are a weekend business: Saturday and Sunday bring 20.0% and 17.9% of in-store orders, when an even week would give 14.3% a day. Online ordering is flatter, with Monday and Sunday on top at 15.7% and 15.6%. The column percentages answer 'of store orders, what share is on a Saturday?'. Row percentages would answer a different question: 'of Saturday's orders, what share is in a store?'.

  • labs/module-06/l2_6-pivot-tables/survey_by_age_starter.py (about 15 min): Which survey question does each age group score lowest, and how many answers are behind each average?

Write what you think each one prints before you run it. Being wrong here is useful: it shows you exactly which rule to look at again.

P1. An empty cell.

t = pd.DataFrame({"pay": ["card", "cash", "card"], "ch": ["web", "shop", "shop"],
                  "n": [1, 1, 1]})
p = t.pivot_table(index="pay", columns="ch", values="n", aggfunc="count")

P2. Labels after a pivot.

t = pd.DataFrame({"month": ["Jan", "Feb", "Mar", "Apr"], "store": ["a"] * 4,
                  "rev": [1, 2, 3, 4]})
print(t.pivot(index="month", columns="store", values="rev").index.tolist())

P3. A margin of means.

t = pd.DataFrame({"store": ["a", "a", "a", "b"], "amt": [10, 10, 10, 50]})
p = t.pivot_table(index="store", values="amt", aggfunc="mean", margins=True)
print(p["amt"].tolist(), t.groupby("store")["amt"].mean().mean())

Glossary for Session 6

  • Key: a column (or set of columns) that appears in two tables and connects their rows.
  • Merge / join: combining two tables side by side by matching rows on a key.
  • Inner join: keeps only keys found in both tables. The default of merge.
  • Left join: keeps every row of the left table; unmatched rows get NaN in the borrowed columns.
  • Outer join: keeps every row of both tables.
  • Indicator: the _merge column added by indicator=True: both, left_only or right_only.
  • Many-to-one: a relationship in which the key is unique in the right table: each left row finds at most one partner.
  • One-to-many: the key is unique in the left table: each left row can match several right rows, so rows multiply.
  • Many-to-many: the key repeats on both sides: every pairing appears. Usually a mistake.
  • validate=: a merge argument that states the expected relationship and raises MergeError if the keys say otherwise.
  • Concatenate: stacking tables on top of each other with pd.concat.
  • Long (tidy) data: one row per observation, one column per variable: the logbook.
  • Wide data: one variable's values spread across the column headers: the scoreboard.
  • Pivot table: a groupby on row and column keys whose result is spread into a grid.
  • Margins: the totals row and column added by margins=True, computed from all the underlying rows.
  • Crosstab: a table of counts of two categorical columns (pd.crosstab); normalize turns counts into shares.
  • Melt: turning column headers back into the values of one column (df.melt): wide to tidy.
Instructor Kit · Participant Workbook

Session 7: Time Series and Windows

Your working notebook for the session where the calendar becomes part of your data. Before the break you turn a column of date text into a ruler, find the days that are missing from two years of store sales, and roll days up into weeks, months and years. After the break you slide windows along that ruler: moving averages, running totals and ranks, and you learn to keep every calculation honest about what it could have known.

Lessons 2.7–2.8 2 lessons Session 7 · 3 hours Name: ____________________
How to use this workbook

Each lesson has Key ideas (the reference to keep), a Worked example with its real output, the Guided lab you do in class, Predict, then run boxes, the start of the lesson project, a six-question Self-check and space for notes. The lab files are in labs/module-07/: open the _starter.py file, run it as it is, then work through the TODOs in order. Every starter runs before you change anything and tells you which TODOs are still open.

Session Goals

  • Turn date text into real dates and select days, months and ranges with a date string
  • Check a time series against the calendar, and decide whether each missing day is a true zero or an unknown
  • Roll daily rows up to weeks, months and years with resample(), and print the count beside every total
  • Measure change with shift(), diff() and pct_change(), including year over year
  • Smooth a series with a rolling window, and build running totals, year-to-date figures and ranks
  • Write a baseline that uses only the past, and explain look-ahead leakage in your own words

Lessons

2.7

Time Series

90 min solo
Key ideas
  • Dates are a ruler, not labels. Text that looks like a date has no order, no distances and no months. Parse it: pd.read_csv(..., parse_dates=["date"]) or pd.to_datetime(column). Check df.dtypes after every load.
  • .dt reads the parts of a date column: .dt.year, .dt.month, .dt.dayofweek (Monday is 0), .dt.day_name(). The new columns are ordinary columns you can group by.
  • Put dates in the index and .loc understands strings: .loc["2025-07-04"] is a day, .loc["2025-07"] a month, .loc["2025"] a year. A string range includes both ends.
  • Check against the calendar. pd.date_range(start, end, freq="D") is every day; calendar.difference(table.index) is the days you do not have; asfreq("D") gives each of them a row of NaN.
  • A gap is a decision. Closed store: a true 0. Export failed or value blank: unknown, so leave it NaN, or estimate it and say so. The data cannot tell you which is which; documentation can.
  • resample() is groupby for the calendar. Sums skip NaN without telling you, so print count beside every sum.
  • Change means "compared with an earlier row". shift(1) shows the previous row, diff() subtracts it, pct_change() divides by it. They count rows, so they mean "earlier in time" only on a complete calendar.
  • The order of operations: parse → index → complete the calendar → decide each gap → then resample, shift or compare.
You wantWrite
Real dates when loadingpd.read_csv(path, parse_dates=["date"])
Every row of one months.loc["2025-07"]
A range, both ends includeds.loc["2024-12-30":"2025-01-02"]
The days that are missingpd.date_range(a, b, freq="D").difference(df.index)
One row per calendar daydf.asfreq("D")
Monthly totals with a checks.resample("ME").agg(["sum", "count"])
Monthly totals from a date columndf.resample("ME", on="order_date")["order_total"].sum()
Per group and per quarter, long tabledf.groupby(["store", pd.Grouper(key="date", freq="QE")])
Growth against last year (monthly rows)monthly.pct_change(12)
Buckets"D" day · "W" week ending Sunday · "ME" month · "QE" quarter · "YE" year
Worked example: how many rainy days, and is the calendar complete?

The weather file has one row per city per day. Before counting anything by month, check the calendar. (This example uses data/store_weather.csv, which the lesson does not, so the numbers are new.)

Parse the dates, go wide, and compare with a full calendar.

weather = pd.read_csv("data/store_weather.csv", parse_dates=["date"])
rain = weather.pivot(index="date", columns="city", values="precip_in")
calendar = pd.date_range(rain.index.min(), rain.index.max(), freq="D")
print(len(rain), len(calendar), len(calendar.difference(rain.index)))
731 731 0

731 rows, 731 days, nothing missing: this table needs no asfreq. Now count the days with at least 0.10 inches of rain in each quarter. A comparison gives True/False, and the sum of True/False values is a count.

rainy = rain >= 0.10
by_quarter = rainy.resample("QE").agg(["sum", "count"])
print(by_quarter["Flagstaff"])
            sum  count
date                  
2024-03-31   15     91
2024-06-30    3     91
2024-09-30   26     92
2024-12-31    9     92
2025-03-31    9     90
2025-06-30    4     91
2025-09-30   25     92
2025-12-31    8     92

count confirms each quarter is complete (90 to 92 days). Flagstaff's rain arrives in the third quarter. Is that the same in both years? Compare each quarter with the same quarter a year earlier: 4 rows back.

print(rainy.resample("QE").sum().diff(4).loc["2025"])
city        Asheville  Bend  Boise  Flagstaff
date                                         
2025-03-31       -3.0   3.0   -5.0       -6.0
2025-06-30        4.0  -2.0    1.0        1.0
2025-09-30        3.0  -1.0    4.0       -1.0
2025-12-31       -2.0   2.0    0.0       -1.0

Read one cell: Asheville had 3 more rainy days in the third quarter of 2025 than in the third quarter of 2024. The pattern for this example was the order of operations: parse, index, check the calendar, then resample and compare.

My notes for this lesson
2.8

Window Functions

90 min solo
Key ideas
  • A window function returns one value for every row, computed from nearby rows. groupby().agg() and resample() return one value per group.
  • rolling(n) is a fixed pane sliding down the rows: this row and the n - 1 before it. Follow it with .mean(), .sum(), .max(), .std(). Match n to the cycle you want to remove: 7 for a weekly rhythm.
  • A window must be full by default. The first n - 1 rows are NaN, and one missing value blanks n results. min_periods= relaxes that. Choose a threshold you can defend (6 of 7, 21 of 28), not 1.
  • center=True puts the row in the middle of its window, so it uses future rows. Fine for describing history; never for predicting or flagging.
  • Expanding windows only grow: cumsum() is a running total, cummax() the best so far, expanding().mean() the average so far. Year to date is a running total within each year: groupby(index.year).cumsum().
  • On a long table: sort, group, window. groupby("store")["revenue"].transform(lambda s: s.rolling(7).mean()) keeps the rows lined up. Quick test: the first rows of each group are NaN.
  • rank() gives positions. ascending=False makes the largest value rank 1; method= settles ties; pct=True gives percentiles; groupby(...).rank() ranks inside each group.
  • Look-ahead leakage: a baseline must not contain the row it judges, or anything after it. Ask of every window: "On that day, could I have known every number in it?" Shift first: s.shift(1).rolling(7).mean().
WindowRemembersGood for
rolling(n)The last n rows, equallyRemoving a known cycle; easy to explain
expanding() / cumsum()Everything so far, equallyRunning totals, year to date, records
ewm(span=n)Everything, recent rows moreA level that drifts; reacting quickly (read the lesson section)
shift(1) then a windowOnly rows before this oneAnything used to predict, flag or score the current row
Worked example: smoothing temperatures, and a record that keeps being broken

Daily high temperatures in Boise jump around from day to day. A window makes the season visible. (This example uses data/store_weather.csv, which has no gaps, so it needs no calendar repair.)

One row per day for Boise, then a trailing 7-day mean. The first six rows have no full window yet.

weather = pd.read_csv("data/store_weather.csv", parse_dates=["date"])
boise_high = weather[weather["city"] == "Boise"].set_index("date")["high_f"]
view = pd.DataFrame({"high_f": boise_high,
                     "avg_7d": boise_high.rolling(7).mean().round(1)})
print(view.head(8))
            high_f  avg_7d
date                      
2024-01-01      37     NaN
2024-01-02      44     NaN
2024-01-03      30     NaN
2024-01-04      26     NaN
2024-01-05      29     NaN
2024-01-06      27     NaN
2024-01-07      32    32.1
2024-01-08      26    30.6

A day sets a record when it equals the hottest day so far. cummax() is the hottest day so far.

records = boise_high[boise_high == boise_high.cummax()]
print(len(records), "record days; the last three:")
print(records.tail(3))
26 record days; the last three:
date
2024-07-02    109
2025-07-12    110
2025-07-13    112
Name: high_f, dtype: int64

Was July 13, 2025 unusual? Compare it with the 7 days before it, not with a window that contains it.

before = boise_high.shift(1).rolling(7).mean()
with_today = boise_high.rolling(7).mean()
day = "2025-07-13"
print(boise_high.loc[day], "| before:", round(before.loc[day], 1),
      "| with today:", round(with_today.loc[day], 1))
112 | before: 88.3 | with today: 93.0

The honest baseline (the seven days before) is lower than the one that includes the day itself, so the hot day stands out more against it. Any day you want to judge has to stay out of its own baseline.
