Glossary

TermMeaning
TimestampOne point in time. A datetime64 column holds one per row; it knows its year, month and weekday.
TimedeltaA length of time: what you get when you subtract two timestamps.
NaT"Not a time": the missing value for dates, as NaN is for numbers.
DatetimeIndexRow labels that are dates. It lets .loc["2024-07"] select a month and makes resample possible.
asfreqGive the table one row for every period ("D" = every day). Rows that did not exist appear as NaN.
ResampleGroupby for the calendar: drop rows into weeks, months or quarters, then aggregate each bucket.
Frequency aliasThe code for a bucket size: "D", "W", "ME", "QE", "YE", "h". The old "M", "Q", "Y", "H" raise an error.
shift / diff / pct_changeThe previous row's value beside this one; this row minus that; that change as a fraction.
Year over yearA period compared with the same period one year earlier: 12 rows back in monthly data, 4 in quarterly.
Window functionA calculation that returns one value for every row, computed from nearby rows.
Rolling windowA fixed-size window that slides down the rows: rolling(7) is this row and the six before it.
min_periodsHow many real values a window needs before it gives an answer. The default is all of them.
Expanding windowA window that starts at the first row and only grows: running totals, best so far.
Year to dateA running total that restarts each January 1: groupby(index.year).cumsum().
RankA value's position in sorted order. method= settles ties; pct=True gives a percentile.
Look-ahead leakageUsing information in a calculation that was not available at the moment the calculation describes.

Lessons 2.9–3.1 2 lessons Session 8 · 3 hours Name: ____________________
How to use this workbook

Each lesson has Key ideas, a Worked example with its real output, the Guided lab you do in class, Predict, then run boxes, the start of the lesson project, a six-question Self-check and space for notes. The lab files are in labs/module-08/. The two chart labs save their pictures as PNG files in an output/ folder next to the lab file: open them from there, or run the file cell by cell in VS Code to see each chart as you go.

Session Goals

  • Decide what one row of a feature table represents, and build flags, ratios, date parts and bins for it
  • Summarize many rows per customer or per order, join the summary back, and fill missing values honestly
  • Encode an ordered category and an unordered one correctly
  • Apply the cutoff rule: every feature must be computable on the cutoff date from what existed then
  • Build any chart with fig, ax = plt.subplots() and methods on ax, with a title and labeled axes
  • Choose line, scatter, bar or histogram from the question, and spot a chart that draws without error and is wrong

Lessons

2.9

Feature Engineering Basics

95 min solo
Key ideas
  • A feature is a column you build so a pattern becomes visible. First decide the unit of analysis: what one row is. Every feature must be one fact about that unit.
  • Derived columns come from four patterns: a flag (.notna(), .isin(), a comparison), arithmetic between columns, a choice (np.where), a lookup (.map). Ratios remove size and leave behavior.
  • Dates become features as parts that repeat (.dt.month, a weekend flag) or as elapsed time from a fixed reference date. Never use today's date.
  • pd.cut: you choose the edges; each bin includes its right edge; values outside the edges become NaN. pd.qcut: equal-sized groups, edges chosen from the data.
  • An ordered categorical (pd.CategoricalDtype([...], ordered=True)) sorts, compares and takes max() in the real order, not the alphabet.
  • Per-customer features: group and aggregate to one row each, then left-merge onto the table that defines the unit. Fill counts and sums with 0. Leave averages missing and add a flag.
  • Encoding: ordinal categories → ordered integers (.cat.codes). Nominal categories → one yes/no column each (pd.get_dummies).
  • The cutoff rule: could I have computed this value on the cutoff date, using only what existed then? If not, it leaks. Leaks raise no error; they give results that are too good.
You wantWrite
A yes/no flagdf["col"].notna() · df["a"] == "x" · df["a"].isin([...])
A two-way choicenp.where(condition, "large", "regular")
Days since a date(as_of - df["date"]).dt.days with as_of = pd.Timestamp("2025-12-31")
Bands with edges you choosepd.cut(s, bins=[17, 24, 34, 120], labels=[...])
Edges that cover everythingbins=[-np.inf, 40, 60, 80, np.inf]
Four equal-sized groupspd.qcut(s, q=4, labels=["low", "mid", "high", "top"])
One row per customerorders.groupby("customer_id").agg(n_orders=("order_id", "count"), ...)
Join without losing anyonebase.merge(summary, on="customer_id", how="left", validate="one_to_one")
One-hot columnspd.get_dummies(df["channel"], prefix="via", dtype=int)
Rate and size per groupdf.groupby("feature")["target"].agg(["mean", "size"])
Worked example: product features: does time on sale explain review counts?

One row is a product. The question: which products have many reviews? Two candidate features: how long the product has been on sale, and how expensive it is. (The lesson builds customer features; this example uses data/products.csv.)

Elapsed time from a fixed reference date, then bands with edges that mean something (under a year, one to three years, longer).

products = pd.read_csv("data/products.csv", parse_dates=["launch_date"])
as_of = pd.Timestamp("2025-12-31")
products["years_on_sale"] = (as_of - products["launch_date"]).dt.days / 365.25
products["age_band"] = pd.cut(products["years_on_sale"], bins=[0, 1, 3, np.inf],
                              labels=["new", "established", "veteran"])
print(products["age_band"].value_counts(sort=False, dropna=False))
age_band
new             13
established     49
veteran        180
Name: count, dtype: int64

No NaN row in that count, so every product landed in a band. Now check the feature against the thing we care about, with the group size beside it.

print(products.groupby("age_band", observed=True)["review_count"]
      .agg(["mean", "median", "size"]).round(1))
             mean  median  size
age_band                       
new          37.5     0.0    13
established  56.8    34.0    49
veteran      76.6    40.5   180

The second feature: three equal-sized price bands, made by qcut.

products["price_band"] = pd.qcut(products["price"], q=3,
                                 labels=["low", "mid", "high"])
print(products.groupby("price_band", observed=True)["review_count"]
      .agg(["mean", "median", "size"]).round(1))
            mean  median  size
price_band                    
low         99.5    61.0    81
mid         56.8    26.0    81
high        54.8    26.5    80

Both features separate the products: review counts rise with time on sale, and the cheapest third of the catalog collects far more reviews than the rest. Notice observed=True: these are categoricals, and it keeps the output to the bands that exist. And notice the 13 new products with a median of 0 reviews: for a product launched last month, "no reviews" says nothing about quality.

Predict 2.9.1 The same three values, binned twice. What does each line print?

v = pd.Series([10, 20, 30])
edges, names = [0, 10, 20, 30], ["a", "b", "c"]
print(pd.cut(v, bins=edges, labels=names).tolist())
print(pd.cut(v, bins=edges, labels=names, right=False).tolist())

Predict 2.9.2 Three customers, one of whom never ordered. What are the two lists?

people = pd.DataFrame({"id": [1, 2, 3]})
bought = pd.DataFrame({"id": [1, 1, 3], "total": [10.0, 20.0, 7.0]})
per = bought.groupby("id").agg(n=("total", "count"), avg=("total", "mean"))
t = people.merge(per, on="id", how="left")
t["n"] = t["n"].fillna(0).astype(int)
print(t["n"].tolist(), t["avg"].tolist())

Predict 2.9.3 What are the column names, and what does each column add up to?

d = pd.get_dummies(pd.Series(["web", "store", "web"]), dtype=int)
print(d.columns.tolist(), d.sum().tolist(), d.sum(axis=1).tolist())

My notes for this lesson
3.1

Line, Scatter and Bar Charts

90 min solo
Key ideas
  • A chart is an answer to a question. Decide the question first; the chart type follows.
  • Figure = the whole canvas (it has a size, and it is what gets saved). Axes = one chart on it. Create both with fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(7, 3.5), layout="constrained").
  • One rule: plt creates figures and shows them. Everything else is a method on ax or fig: ax.plot, ax.set_title, ax.set_xlabel, ax.legend, fig.savefig.
  • Most of a chart is made in pandas first. Aggregate to the level the question needs, then plot.
  • Line (ax.plot): change over time. One call per line; label= feeds ax.legend().
  • Scatter (ax.scatter): do two numbers move together? A small s and an alpha below 1 fix overplotting. It shows a pattern, not a cause.
  • Bar (ax.bar, ax.barh): which group is bigger? ax.bar never aggregates: one number per bar, and you compute it. Bars start at zero.
  • Histogram (ax.hist): what is typical, how spread out? It always counts: give it the raw rows. bins and range are your decisions; say what you left out.
Your questionChartMethodPrepare with
How did it change over time?Lineax.plot(x, y)resample or group by a period
Do two numbers move together?Scatterax.scatter(x, y, s=8, alpha=0.3)Usually nothing: plot the rows
Which group is bigger?Barax.bar(names, values) · ax.barhgroupby or value_counts, then sort
What is typical? How spread out?Histogramax.hist(column, bins=40)Nothing: it counts for you
You see plt.title(...)ax.set_title(...)
You see plt.savefig(...)fig.savefig(...)
Worked example: how did the number of orders per month change, online and in stores?

The question is about change over time, so the answer is a line chart, and most of the work happens in pandas before any plotting. (The lesson charts store revenue; this example counts orders by channel.)

Aggregate first: one row per month, one column per channel, each cell a count of orders.

orders = pd.read_csv("data/orders.csv", parse_dates=["order_date"])
orders["month"] = orders["order_date"].dt.to_period("M").dt.to_timestamp()
per_month = orders.pivot_table(index="month", columns="channel",
                               values="order_id", aggfunc="count")
print(per_month.iloc[[0, -1]])
channel     online  store
month                    
2024-01-01     409    204
2025-12-01     769    376

Then the five-line pattern, with one ax.plot call per column. A solid line and a dashed line, so the chart still works without color.

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 3.6), layout="constrained")
ax.plot(per_month.index, per_month["online"], marker="o", markersize=3,
        color="#667eea", label="Online")
ax.plot(per_month.index, per_month["store"], marker="s", markersize=3,
        color="#d95f02", linestyle="--", label="Store (account holders)")
ax.set_title("Orders per month by channel")
ax.set_ylabel("Number of orders")
ax.set_ylim(bottom=0)
ax.legend()
plt.show()
print(len(fig.axes), len(ax.lines))
1 2

Both channels grow, and both spike in December. Online runs at about twice the store count throughout. The label on the store line matters: orders.csv holds store orders by signed-in account holders only, and a chart should say what it is counting.

Glossary

TermMeaning
FeatureA column you build so that a pattern becomes visible: orders so far, days since last order, share bought online.
Unit of analysisWhat one row of a table represents. The question decides it: a customer, an order, a store-day.
Derived columnA column computed from other columns in the same row: a flag, a difference, a ratio, a choice.
Reference dateA fixed date, written down, from which elapsed time is measured. Never today's date.
BinningTurning a number into a group. pd.cut: you choose the edges. pd.qcut: equal-sized groups from the data.
CategoricalA column that can hold only values from a fixed list. Ordered categoricals sort and compare correctly.
Ordinal / nominalA category with a meaningful order (tiers, age bands) / one without (store, channel).
One-hot encodingOne yes/no column per category, made with pd.get_dummies. For nominal categories.
ScalingPutting numeric columns on a common footing: min-max (0 to 1), z-score (mean 0, std 1), np.log1p for skew.
Cutoff dateThe date you pretend to stand on. Features come from before it; the target comes from after it.
LeakageA feature that uses information not available on the cutoff date. No error; results that are too good.
FigureThe whole picture: the sheet of paper. It has a size and it is what gets saved.
AxesOne chart on a Figure, with its own x axis, y axis, title and data. Not the plural of axis.
OverplottingSo many dots on top of each other that density is invisible. Fix with a small s and alpha.
Bin (histogram)One of the equal intervals a histogram cuts the range into; the bar shows how many rows fall in it.
Object-oriented interfaceDrawing with methods on ax and fig. plt is used only to create and show figures.
Instructor Kit · Participant Workbook

Session 9: Composing and Customizing Figures

Your working notebook for the session where charts become figures for other people. Before the break you put several charts on one page: grids, shared scales, named layouts, and an honest alternative to the two-axis chart. After the break you edit one chart from Matplotlib's first draft to a finished figure: a headline that states the finding, readable axes, color that points at the story, labels where the eye already is, and nothing that does not earn its place.

How to use this workbook

Each lesson has Key ideas, a Worked example with its real output and chart, the Guided lab you do in class, Predict, then run boxes, the start of the lesson project, a six-question Self-check and space for notes. The lab files are in labs/module-09/. Every lab saves its charts as PNG files in an output/ folder next to the lab file: open them there, or run the file cell by cell in VS Code to see each figure as you go.

Session Goals

  • Create a grid of charts with plt.subplots(rows, cols), loop over it with zip(axs.flat, ...), and title the whole figure
  • Decide whether panels share a scale, and tell the reader when they do not
  • Build an uneven layout with plt.subplot_mosaic and fill each panel by name
  • Replace a twin-axis chart of two different measures with stacked panels or a ratio
  • Edit a default chart: a finding headline checked with a number, formatted ticks, gray plus one, direct labels, an annotation, no junk
  • Capture repeated choices in a house style with plt.rc_context

Lessons

3.2

Subplots and Composition

85 min solo
Key ideas
  • Panels on a page. The Figure is the page; each Axes is a panel. The arrangement tells the reader how to read them.
  • plt.subplots(rows, cols) returns an array of Axes: 2-D for a grid, 1-D for one row or column, a single Axes for 1 by 1. squeeze=False always gives 2-D.
  • Loop, don't repeat: for ax, item in zip(axs.flat, items): works for every grid shape.
  • sharey=True makes heights comparable; separate scales show each shape and hide differences in size. Choose for the question, and say so when panels do not share.
  • Label the figure, not four panels: fig.suptitle, fig.supylabel, fig.supxlabel. Use layout="constrained" so nothing collides.
  • plt.subplot_mosaic builds an uneven layout from a picture of names and returns a dictionary: axd["trend"]. A repeated name spans cells.
  • A twin axis with two different measures can be made to say anything. Stack panels with sharex=True, or plot the ratio. A second axis is fine for one quantity in two units (secondary_yaxis).
  • Small multiples: one function that draws a panel on the ax it is given, plus a loop. Gray for the other groups, color for the panel's own.
You ask forYou getIndex it with
plt.subplots()A single Axesax
plt.subplots(1, 3) or (3, 1)A 1-D arrayaxs[0], axs[2]
plt.subplots(2, 3)A 2-D arrayaxs[row, col]
plt.subplots(1, 3, squeeze=False)A 2-D array of shape (1, 3)axs[0, 2]
plt.subplot_mosaic([["a", "a"], ["b", "c"]])A dictionaryaxd["a"]
plt.subplot_mosaic("AAB;CCB")A dictionary (one letter per cell)axd["B"]
Worked example: four cities' temperatures as small multiples

Question: how does each store city's year of temperatures compare with the others? Four groups, the same chart for each: small multiples on a shared scale, with the other cities in gray behind each panel. (The lesson draws store revenue; this example uses data/store_weather.csv.)

One row per month, one column per city: the shape that one line per panel wants.

weather = pd.read_csv("data/store_weather.csv", parse_dates=["date"])
weather["month"] = weather["date"].dt.to_period("M").dt.to_timestamp()
highs = weather.pivot_table(index="month", columns="city", values="high_f",
                            aggfunc="mean")
print(highs.round(1).iloc[[0, 6]])
city        Asheville  Bend  Boise  Flagstaff
month                                        
2024-01-01       49.5  37.5   28.5       35.3
2024-07-01       82.4  78.0   95.0       83.3

A function that draws one panel on the Axes it is given, then a loop over a 2 by 2 grid that shares both axes.

def draw_city(ax, table, city):
    for other in table.columns:
        if other != city:
            ax.plot(table.index, table[other], color="lightgray", linewidth=1)
    ax.plot(table.index, table[city], color="#667eea", linewidth=2)
    ax.set_title(city)

fig, axs = plt.subplots(2, 2, figsize=(9, 5), sharex=True, sharey=True,
                        layout="constrained")
for ax, city in zip(axs.flat, highs.columns):
    draw_city(ax, highs, city)
fig.suptitle("Mean daily high by month: each city against the other three")
fig.supylabel("Mean daily high (°F)")
plt.show()
print(axs.shape, [len(ax.lines) for ax in axs.flat])
(2, 2) [4, 4, 4, 4]

Every panel holds four lines, and only one is in color. Because the scale is shared, you can see at once that Boise has the widest swing between winter and summer (its mean monthly high runs from about 28 °F in January 2024 to 95 °F in July 2024) and Asheville the narrowest. With separate scales the four shapes would look almost alike.

My notes for this lesson
3.3

Customizing Plots

90 min solo
Key ideas
  • A default chart is a first draft; you are the editor. Every edit should help the reader get the point faster.
  • The title states the finding; the subtitle says what is plotted, the units and the period. Check the finding with a number before you write it, and keep causes out unless they were tested.
  • Locators choose where ticks go; formatters what they say: "${x:,.0f}", FuncFormatter(thousands), PercentFormatter(xmax=1), mdates.MonthLocator, mdates.DateFormatter("%b %Y").
  • Give color one job: highlight (gray plus one), categories (qualitative, such as Okabe-Ito), magnitude (sequential, such as viridis), above or below a midpoint (diverging, centered).
  • Design for color-blind readers: a safe palette, no red against green as the only difference, and never color as the only cue.
  • Direct labels beat a legend when lines can be labeled at their ends: nothing to look up.
  • Annotate sparingly: ax.annotate with an arrow, ax.axvspan for a period, ax.axhline for a reference. One to three per chart.
  • Delete chart junk (extra spines, tick marks, heavy grids, repeated labels). Bars start at zero. Put repeated choices in a style dictionary and use it with plt.rc_context.
You wantWrite
A finding headline and a gray subtitleax.set_title(h, loc="left", fontweight="bold", pad=24) + ax.text(0, 1.03, s, transform=ax.transAxes, color="0.35")
Dollars with commasax.yaxis.set_major_formatter("${x:,.0f}")
Thousands of dollarsFuncFormatter(lambda x, pos: f"${x / 1000:,.0f}K")
A fraction as a percentax.yaxis.set_major_formatter(PercentFormatter(xmax=1))
Ticks every January and Julyax.xaxis.set_major_locator(mdates.MonthLocator(bymonth=[1, 7]))
Positions and labels togetherax.set_xticks(range(1, 13), labels=MONTHS)
A label at the end of a lineax.annotate(name, xy=(x, y), xytext=(6, 0), textcoords="offset points")
A shaded periodax.axvspan(start, end, color="0.93", zorder=0)
No top or right frameax.spines[["top", "right"]].set_visible(False)
A style for one blockwith plt.rc_context(pinecrest_style): ...
Worked example: one shrinking store, said in five seconds

The finding from Session 7: three stores grew in 2025 and one shrank. A finished chart should say that before anyone reads the axis. (The lesson edits a line chart; this example edits a bar chart.)

The numbers first: revenue growth per store, 2025 against 2024, sorted.

daily = pd.read_csv("data/daily_sales.csv", parse_dates=["date"])
year = daily["date"].dt.year
yearly = daily.groupby(["store", year])["revenue"].sum().unstack()
growth = ((yearly[2025] / yearly[2024] - 1) * 100).sort_values()
print(growth.round(1))
store
Asheville    -5.0
Bend          3.8
Boise         9.3
Flagstaff    19.7
dtype: float64

Horizontal bars, gray plus one accent for the store that shrank, each value written on its bar, a zero line, a finding headline, and the junk removed.

colors = ["#d55e00" if g < 0 else "0.75" for g in growth]
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 3.4), layout="constrained")
bars = ax.barh(growth.index, growth.values, color=colors)
ax.bar_label(bars, labels=[f"{g:+.1f}%" for g in growth], padding=4)
ax.axvline(0, color="0.3", linewidth=1)
ax.set_title("Asheville is the only store that shrank in 2025", loc="left",
             fontweight="bold", pad=22)
ax.text(0, 1.03, "Revenue change, 2025 against 2024 (register totals)",
        transform=ax.transAxes, color="0.35")
ax.xaxis.set_visible(False)
ax.spines[["top", "right", "bottom"]].set_visible(False)
ax.tick_params(length=0)
ax.set_xlim(growth.min() * 1.6, growth.max() * 1.25)
plt.show()
print([b.get_facecolor()[:3] != (0.75, 0.75, 0.75) for b in bars])
[True, False, False, False]

The printed list marks which bars are not gray: only the first, Asheville. The labels make the x axis unnecessary, so it is hidden; the line at zero is the one reference the reader needs. The headline is a claim the bars prove: -5.0% for Asheville, growth everywhere else.

Glossary

TermMeaning
SubplotsSeveral Axes on one Figure. plt.subplots(nrows, ncols) returns the Figure and an array of Axes.
axs.flatWalks through every panel of the array row by row, whatever its shape.
Squeezeplt.subplots drops dimensions of length one: one row gives a 1-D array, one panel a single Axes. squeeze=False keeps 2-D.
Shared axessharex=True / sharey=True: panels use one scale, so positions compare across panels.
suptitle / supylabelA title or axis label for the whole figure, not one panel.
subplot_mosaicAn uneven layout drawn as a picture of names; returns a dictionary of Axes.
Twin axisax.twinx(): a second y axis on the right with its own, arbitrary scale.
Secondary axisax.secondary_yaxis("right", functions=(f, g)): the same quantity in a second unit, tied by a formula.
Small multiplesThe same small chart repeated once per group, with the same axes.
Finding titleA title that states what the chart shows, with a subtitle that says what is plotted.
Locator / formatterWhere the ticks go / what text each tick shows.
Gray plus oneOne series in color, the rest in gray as context: color used to direct attention.
Qualitative / sequential / divergingPalettes for unordered groups / low-to-high numbers / numbers with a meaningful midpoint.
Direct labelA series' name written next to the series itself, replacing a legend.
Chart junkInk that carries no information: extra spines, tick marks, heavy grids, redundant labels.
rcParams / rc_contextMatplotlib's defaults, and a with block that changes them for the charts drawn inside it only.

Lessons

3.4

Saving Figures

75 min alone
By the end you can
  • Save a figure with fig.savefig() and predict its size in pixels from figsize and dpi
  • Choose between raster formats (PNG) and vector formats (SVG, PDF) for a given destination
  • Explain why text looks tiny or huge in a saved chart, and fix it with the figure size
  • Control the saved area and background with bbox_inches, facecolor and transparent
  • Write one function that applies a house style and saves for web, slides or print, then use it in a loop that closes each figure
Key ideas
  • The sheet and the scanner. figsize is the sheet of paper, in inches. dpi is the scanner. pixels = inches x dpi, once for the width and once for the height.
  • Say which figure. fig.savefig(path) saves the figure you name. plt.savefig() saves whatever pyplot thinks is current, which can be an empty one.
  • The format comes from the extension. .png is a grid of pixels (raster). .svg and .pdf are drawing instructions (vector) and stay sharp at any size.
  • Text is sized in points, and a point is 1/72 of an inch on the sheet. A smaller sheet makes the same text look bigger. Raising the dpi never does.
  • Destinations. Web or email: PNG, about 110 dpi. A 16:9 slide: PNG at 9.6 x 5.4 inches and 200 dpi (1,920 x 1,080). Print or anything that will be resized: PDF or SVG. Never JPEG for charts.
  • bbox_inches="tight" stops labels being cut off and changes the saved size. With layout="constrained" you rarely need it.
  • transparent=True removes the background only. Black text on a dark slide vanishes. A solid facecolor="white" is the safe default.
  • Presets and one save function. Put sizes, dpi and formats in one dictionary; create the figure at the right size before drawing; save through one function.
  • Create, draw, save, close. In a loop, plt.close(fig) after every save. PdfPages puts many figures in one PDF.
  • Do not trust, check. plt.imread(path).shape is (rows, columns, channels). Then open the file at the size it will really be shown.
Worked example

The intranet home page has a banner slot exactly 1,200 pixels wide and 300 high. Work backward: 8 inches x 150 dpi = 1,200 and 2 inches x 150 dpi = 300. Create the figure at that size, draw, save, close, check.

from pathlib import Path
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import pandas as pd

daily = pd.read_csv("data/daily_sales.csv", parse_dates=["date"])
weekly = daily.set_index("date")["visitors"].resample("W").sum()

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 2), layout="constrained")
ax.plot(weekly.index, weekly.values, color="#667eea")
ax.set_title("All stores: visitors per week", loc="left")

Path("charts").mkdir(exist_ok=True)
banner = Path("charts") / "visitors_banner.png"
fig.savefig(banner, dpi=150, facecolor="white")     # 8 x 150 by 2 x 150
plt.close(fig)

print(plt.imread(banner).shape)
print("open figures:", len(plt.get_fignums()))
(300, 1200, 4)
open figures: 0

300 rows, 1,200 columns, 4 values per pixel (red, green, blue, alpha). Zero open figures, because the figure was closed after saving. The same 1,200 x 300 pixels from a 12 x 3 inch sheet at 100 dpi would have a title two thirds the size.

    fig, ax = plt.subplots()
    fig.savefig(f"charts/p2_{i}.png")
print(len(plt.get_fignums()))
plt.close("all")
print(len(plt.get_fignums()))

4.1

Descriptive Statistics I: Center and Spread

85 min alone
By the end you can
  • Compute the mean, median and mode of a column with pandas and say what each one measures
  • Recognize when the mean misleads, and choose the median instead, using Pinecrest's skewed order totals
  • Compute range, variance, standard deviation and interquartile range (IQR), and explain each in the units of the data
  • Explain the difference between dividing by n and by n - 1, and predict which one NumPy and pandas use by default
  • Pair the right measure of center with the right measure of spread for a given column
Key ideas
  • Two questions about any column. Center: what is typical? Spread: how much does it vary? One number is never enough.
  • Mean = sum / count: the balance point. It uses every value, multiplies back to the total, and is pulled by extremes.
  • Median = the middle value of the sorted list. Half the values are below it. It is robust: an extreme value cannot move it far.
  • Mode = the most common value. The only center for categories. .mode() returns a Series because ties happen; take [0] for one value.
  • The mean of a True/False column is a proportion. (totals < totals.mean()).mean() is the share of orders below the mean: 0.626.
  • Right-skewed data (a long tail to the right, like anything about money) has its mean above its median. The gap is information, not an error.
  • Variance = the average squared distance from the mean. Standard deviation (SD) = its square root, back in the data's own units: "a typical distance from the mean".
  • n or n - 1? NumPy divides by n (ddof=0, a population). pandas divides by n - 1 (ddof=1, a sample). If unsure, ddof=1.
  • IQR = Q3 - Q1, the width of the middle half of the data. Robust, like the median.
  • Two standard pairs. Symmetric data: mean and SD. Skewed data, or data that may hold errors: median and IQR. A histogram tells you which you have.
  • Missing values. pandas skips them (print .count() next to len()); np.mean returns nan without an error.
Worked example

Two columns from the product catalog, summarized the same way: the star rating (1 decimal) and the number of reviews.

import pandas as pd

products = pd.read_csv("data/products.csv")
for column in ["rating", "review_count"]:
    values = products[column]
    iqr = values.quantile(0.75) - values.quantile(0.25)
    gap = (values.mean() - values.median()) / values.median() * 100
    print(column, "| n", values.count())
    print(f"  mean {values.mean():.2f}  median {values.median():.2f}")
    print(f"  mean vs median {gap:+.1f}%")
    print(f"  SD {values.std():.2f}  IQR {iqr:.2f}")
rating | n 234
  mean 4.14  median 4.10
  mean vs median +0.9%
  SD 0.39  IQR 0.50
review_count | n 242
  mean 70.45  median 39.00
  mean vs median +80.7%
  SD 88.57  IQR 75.75

Read the gap first (how far the mean sits above the median, as a percentage of the median). For rating the mean and the median almost agree (the mean is 0.9% higher), so there is no long tail: report mean and SD, "ratings average 4.14 with an SD of 0.39". For review_count the mean is 81% above the median: a few products have hundreds of reviews. Report median and IQR, "a typical product has 39 reviews; the middle half have between 14 and about 90". Notice also n: 8 products have no rating yet, and pandas left them out without saying so.

Glossary

  • dpi: dots per inch: how many pixels the saved image has for each inch of the figure.
  • figsize: the size of the figure in inches, (width, height). The sheet of paper.
  • Raster image: a grid of colored pixels with a fixed resolution (PNG, JPEG).
  • Vector image: a list of drawing instructions that stays sharp at any size (SVG, PDF).
  • Point: the unit of font sizes and line widths: 1/72 of an inch on the sheet.
  • Bounding box: the area of the figure that is saved. bbox_inches="tight" fits it to what is drawn, which changes the size.
  • facecolor: the figure's background color. transparent=True removes it.
  • Preset: a named set of saved settings (size, dpi, format) for one destination.
  • Manifest: a small table listing every file you produced, used to verify an export.
  • Center (central tendency): what is typical in a column: mean, median or mode.
  • Spread (variability): how much the values vary: range, variance, SD or IQR.
  • Mean: the sum divided by the count; the balance point of the data.
  • Median: the middle value of the sorted data; half the values are below it.
  • Mode: the most common value; the only center for categories.
  • Robust: barely affected by extreme values. The median and the IQR are robust; the mean and the SD are not.
  • Right-skewed: having a long tail of large values to the right; the mean sits above the median.
  • Range: the largest value minus the smallest.
  • Variance: the average squared deviation from the mean.
  • Standard deviation (SD): the square root of the variance, in the data's own units; a typical distance from the mean.
  • ddof: "delta degrees of freedom": the number subtracted from n before dividing. NumPy's default is 0, pandas's is 1.
  • Population / sample: all of the group you care about / a part of it used to estimate the whole.
  • Quartiles, IQR: Q1 and Q3 have a quarter and three quarters of the values below them; the IQR is Q3 - Q1, the width of the middle half.

Lessons

4.2

Descriptive Statistics II: Shape, Position and Outliers

90 min alone
By the end you can
  • Describe the shape of a distribution with a histogram and a skewness value, and say what the shape implies for your choice of summary
  • Locate a value within its column using percentiles, quantiles and z-scores
  • Apply the IQR and z-score outlier rules, explain why they disagree, and choose a defensible action for flagged values
  • Read every line of describe() and every part of a box plot
  • Summarize by group with groupby().agg(), and summarize 1-to-5 survey answers as the ordered categories they are
Key ideas
  • Four things to know about a column: center, spread, shape, and one value's position in it. Shape decides which summaries are fair.
  • Skewness (.skew()): 0 for a symmetric pile, positive for a long right tail. Between -0.5 and 0.5: mean and SD are fair. Beyond 1: median and IQR. It is not robust and does not show humps: always look at a histogram.
  • A logarithm tames a right tail. Order totals: skew 5.48; np.log(totals): -0.41.
  • Percentiles and quantiles locate by rank. totals.quantile(0.90) takes a fraction (0 to 1). np.percentile(totals, 90) takes 0 to 100. The reverse question: (totals <= 500).mean().
  • A z-score locates by distance: z = (value - mean) / SD. It makes different scales comparable. Standardizing gives mean 0 and SD 1 and does not change the shape.
  • Two outlier rules. IQR fences: below Q1 - 1.5 x IQR or above Q3 + 1.5 x IQR. z-scores: beyond 3. They disagree, because the mean and SD are inflated by the values being judged.
  • A flag is a question, not a verdict. Provable error? Fix or remove it, and note what you did. Real and part of your question? Keep it and use the median and IQR. Real but a different kind of thing? Analyze separately and say so.
  • describe(): count (non-missing), mean, std (sample SD), min, 25%, 50%, 75%, max. Compare mean with 50%, and the two halves of the box.
  • A box plot: the line is the median; the box is Q1 to Q3; each whisker ends at the last data point inside the fence; separate points are the flagged values. Good for comparing groups; hides humps.
  • Grouped summaries always carry their n. count is the number of values, size the number of rows.
  • Survey answers on a 1-to-5 scale are ordinal: ordered categories. Lead with the distribution, the median and the share satisfied (4 or 5), and check who did not answer.
Worked example

Was Black Friday 2025 an unusual day for foot traffic at the Flagstaff store? Locate it two ways: by distance (a z-score) and by rank (a percentile).

import pandas as pd

daily = pd.read_csv("data/daily_sales.csv", parse_dates=["date"])
flagstaff = daily.loc[daily["store"] == "Flagstaff"].set_index("date")
visitors = flagstaff["visitors"]

day = visitors.loc["2025-11-28"]                  # Black Friday 2025
z = (day - visitors.mean()) / visitors.std()
rank = (visitors <= day).mean()

print("visitors that day:", day)
print("store mean:", round(visitors.mean(), 1))
print("store SD  :", round(visitors.std(), 1))
print("z-score:", round(z, 2))
print(f"at or below this on {rank:.1%} of Flagstaff's days")

boise = daily.loc[daily["store"] == "Boise", "visitors"]
print("the same count in Boise: z =",
      round((day - boise.mean()) / boise.std(), 2))
visitors that day: 596
store mean: 328.5
store SD  : 117.4
z-score: 2.28
at or below this on 96.8% of Flagstaff's days
the same count in Boise: z = 0.68

596 visitors is 2.28 standard deviations above Flagstaff's average, and only about 3 days in 100 were busier. The same 596 visitors in Boise would be 0.68 SDs above average: a good day, nothing more. A z-score is always measured against a particular group's mean and SD, so say which group.

4.3

Probability Distributions

100 min alone
By the end you can
  • Explain what a random variable and a probability distribution are, and tell discrete from continuous
  • Choose between the uniform, normal, binomial and Poisson distributions for a situation, and check the choice against data
  • Answer probability questions with scipy.stats using pmf/pdf, cdf, sf and ppf
  • Simulate random outcomes reproducibly with np.random.default_rng and use simulation to check a calculation
  • Demonstrate the central limit theorem by simulation, compute a standard error, and build and correctly interpret a 95% confidence interval for a mean
Key ideas
  • A random variable is a number decided by chance. Its distribution lists the possible values with their probabilities: the vending machine's stocking list.
  • Discrete variables are counted (returns in a day). Continuous ones are measured (spend per visitor), and only ranges have probability.
  • Four workhorses. Uniform: equally likely anywhere in a range. Normal: sums and averages of many small effects (mean, SD). Binomial: successes in n independent tries with the same p. Poisson: events in an interval at a steady rate (mean about equal to variance).
  • Freeze, then ask. model = stats.norm(loc=mean, scale=sd). cdf(x): this or less. sf(x): more than this. ppf(q): which value has q below it. pmf(k): exactly k (discrete). rvs(size, random_state=rng): random draws.
  • scale is the SD, not the variance. For stats.uniform, loc is the start and scale is the width.
  • For a count, "5 or more" is sf(4).
  • Simulation checks any formula. Create one seeded generator at the top (rng = np.random.default_rng(42)) and keep drawing from it. Never create it inside a loop.
  • 68-95-99.7: in a normal distribution about 68% of values are within 1 SD of the mean, 95% within 2, 99.7% within 3. It is a property of the normal shape only.
  • A model's assumptions are part of it. Check the fit against data before trusting its answers.
  • Central limit theorem: means of random samples are approximately normal, centered on the population mean, with SD sigma / sqrt(n), whatever the population's shape. The data itself does not become normal.
  • Standard error: SE = s / sqrt(n): how much a sample mean varies from sample to sample. The SD describes single values and does not shrink with more data.
  • 95% confidence interval: mean plus or minus about 2 SE (stats.t.interval). The 95% describes the method: about 95 of 100 intervals built this way contain the true mean. It is not a range for individual values.
Worked example

About 7% of Pinecrest's orders are paid with a gift card. Out of the next 30 orders, how many will be? A fixed number of tries (30), each with the same probability: a binomial model. Compute, then check by simulation.

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from scipy import stats

rng = np.random.default_rng(3)
orders = pd.read_csv("data/orders.csv")
p_gift = (orders["payment_method"] == "gift_card").mean()

batch = stats.binom(n=30, p=p_gift)               # gift-card orders out of 30
print("share paid by gift card:", round(p_gift, 4))
print("expected in a batch of 30:", round(batch.mean(), 2))
print(f"P(none)      = {batch.pmf(0):.3f}")
print(f"P(5 or more) = {batch.sf(4):.3f}")

sim = rng.binomial(n=30, p=p_gift, size=100_000)  # check by counting
print("simulated P(5 or more):", round((sim >= 5).mean(), 3))
share paid by gift card: 0.074
expected in a batch of 30: 2.22
P(none)      = 0.100
P(5 or more) = 0.067
simulated P(5 or more): 0.067

About 2 gift-card orders per batch on average; one batch in ten has none; five or more happens in about 7% of batches. "5 or more" is sf(4): more than 4. The simulation, which only counts, agrees with the formula to the third decimal. The model assumes each order is independent with the same probability, which is worth doubting in December.

Predict 4.3-1
Two numbers. No calculator needed.
model = stats.norm(loc=50, scale=10)
print(model.cdf(50), model.ppf(0.5))
Predict 4.3-2
Ten coin flips. Three numbers: predict each.
flips = stats.binom(n=10, p=0.5)
print(flips.mean(), flips.cdf(10), flips.sf(10))

Glossary

  • Distribution: how the values of a column (or the outcomes of a random variable) are spread: which values occur and how often.
  • Skewness: a number for lopsidedness: 0 for symmetric, positive for a long right tail, negative for a long left tail.
  • Percentile / quantile: the value with a given share of the data at or below it, on a 0-to-100 scale (percentile) or a 0-to-1 scale (quantile).
  • z-score: (value - mean) / SD: how many standard deviations a value lies above or below the mean.
  • Outlier: a value that sits far from the rest of its column. A candidate for a closer look, not automatically an error.
  • IQR fences: Q1 - 1.5 x IQR and Q3 + 1.5 x IQR. Values beyond them are flagged; box plots use this rule.
  • Box plot: a drawing of the median, the quartiles, whiskers to the last points inside the fences, and the flagged points.
  • Ordinal data: ordered categories with no guaranteed distances between them, such as 1-to-5 survey answers (a Likert item).
  • Top-two-box: the share of answers that are 4 or 5 on a 1-to-5 scale.
  • Missing not at random: missing for a reason connected to what is being measured, so the answers you have may not represent the group.
  • Random variable: a number whose value is decided by chance.
  • Discrete / continuous: counted in separate values / measured on a continuous range.
  • Expected value: the long-run average of a random variable.
  • Frozen distribution: a scipy.stats distribution with its parameters fixed, ready to answer questions.
  • pmf / pdf: the probability of exactly k (discrete) / the height of the curve at x (continuous; not a probability).
  • cdf / sf / ppf: the probability of x or less / of more than x / the value with probability q below it.
  • Normal distribution: the bell curve, described by its mean and SD.
  • Binomial distribution: the number of successes in n independent tries with the same probability p.
  • Poisson distribution: the number of events in a fixed interval at a steady average rate; its mean equals its variance.
  • Overdispersion: more day-to-day variation than a single-rate Poisson allows: variance well above the mean.
  • Central limit theorem (CLT): means of random samples are approximately normal, centered on the population mean, with SD sigma / sqrt(n).
  • Standard error (SE): s / sqrt(n): how much a sample mean varies from sample to sample.
  • Confidence interval: an estimate with a margin of error, from a method that captures the true value a stated share of the time (95%).

Lessons

4.4

Correlation Analysis

95 min alone
By the end you can
  • Read direction, form and strength from a scatter plot, and explain why the plot comes before the number
  • Compute Pearson and Spearman correlations with pandas and scipy, and choose between them
  • Build a correlation matrix and draw it as an annotated heatmap with Matplotlib
  • Identify a confounding variable and test for it by comparing like with like
  • Fit and interpret a simple regression line, and state what it does and does not tell you
Key ideas
  • Plot first. A scatter plot shows direction (rising or falling), form (straight, curved, clusters) and strength (how tightly points hug the form). r summarizes one kind of pattern only.
  • Pearson's r measures straight-line association from -1 to +1. It is the average product of z-scores: no units, symmetric, and sensitive to extreme points. x.corr(y).
  • r is not a slope and not a percentage. The share of variance a straight line accounts for is r squared: r = 0.5 is 25%.
  • r = 0 means no linear trend, not no relationship. A perfect curve can score 0.
  • Spearman's rho is Pearson on ranks (method="spearman"): consistent up or down, straight or not. Use it for curves, outliers and ordinal data. A gap between the two means a curve or an influential point. Compute both.
  • A correlation matrix (df[cols].corr()) shows every pair. A block of high correlations usually has one common driver. Heatmaps: a diverging colormap with the scale fixed at -1 to 1.
  • Correlation is not causation. A causes B, B causes A, a confounder causes both, selection, or chance. Ask how the data came to be.
  • A confounder drives both variables. Test for it by comparing like with like: subtract each group's mean (groupby().transform("mean")) and correlate the deviations, or use a partial correlation.
  • The staffing verdict: staff and revenue r = 0.88 pooled, 0.14 among comparable days, 0.07 holding visitors constant. The calendar drives both.
  • A regression line (stats.linregress(x, y), predictor first) gives the slope in real units. Predict only within the data's range. A slope describes; it is not the effect of changing x.
Worked example

Do orders with more product lines cost more? Measure two ways, then fit a line to get the answer in dollars. (No test here: whether a pattern could be chance is Lesson 4.5's question.)

import pandas as pd
from scipy import stats

orders = pd.read_csv("data/orders.csv")
items = pd.read_csv("data/order_items.csv")
lines = items.groupby("order_id").size().rename("lines")
both = orders.merge(lines, on="order_id")

print("orders:", len(both))
print("Pearson :", round(both["lines"].corr(both["order_total"]), 3))
print("Spearman:", round(both["lines"].corr(both["order_total"],
                                            method="spearman"), 3))
fit = stats.linregress(both["lines"], both["order_total"])
print("slope   :", round(fit.slope, 2), "dollars per extra product line")
print("r squared:", round(fit.rvalue ** 2, 3))
orders: 20302
Pearson : 0.54
Spearman: 0.646
slope   : 86.46 dollars per extra product line
r squared: 0.291

Spearman (0.65) is clearly above Pearson (0.54): the relationship is consistent but not a straight band, because a few orders with one bulk line are very large. Each extra product line goes with about $86 more, on average, and the line accounts for about 30% of the variance: the number of lines is far from the whole story (what is on them matters more). Notice the reading: "goes with", not "adds".

4.5

Hypothesis Testing

110 min alone
By the end you can
  • Explain the logic of a hypothesis test in plain words, and say what a p-value is and what it is not
  • Build a permutation test by simulation and match it to SciPy's ready-made tests
  • Choose and run a one-sample, Welch two-sample or paired t-test, a chi-square test, or a rank-based test for the data in front of you
  • Report every test with an effect size and a confidence interval, and check its assumptions on the data
  • Describe Type I and Type II errors and power, and correct for multiple comparisons
Key ideas
  • A hypothesis test asks one question: how surprising would these data be if nothing were going on?
  • The four steps: state what "nothing going on" means (the null, H0); work out what that would produce; see where the real result falls; report how rare it is.
  • The p-value is the probability, if the null hypothesis were true, of a result at least as extreme as the one observed. It is not the probability that the null is true, and it is not a measure of size.
  • Verdicts: p below alpha (usually 0.05, chosen before looking): reject the null. Otherwise: fail to reject. Never "accept". 0.049 and 0.051 are the same evidence: report the number.
  • A permutation test builds "nothing going on" by shuffling the labels many times. The named tests reach the same answer with theory.
  • Choosing: two independent groups: Welch (ttest_ind(x, y, equal_var=False)). One group against a target: ttest_1samp. The same units twice: paired (ttest_rel). Counts in categories: chi-square (chi2_contingency(crosstab)). Ratings or ranks: Mann-Whitney (mannwhitneyu).
  • Every p-value travels with an effect size and a confidence interval. Cohen's d (0.2 small, 0.5 medium, 0.8 large); for tables, the rates and their ratio.
  • Statistically significant = unlikely to be chance alone. Practically significant = big enough to matter. Large samples make tiny effects significant.
  • Two ways to be wrong: a false alarm (Type I, rate alpha) and a miss (Type II). Power is the chance of catching a real effect; it rises with sample size. "Not significant" means "not detected".
  • Many tests, many false alarms. Count every test you ran, correct with Holm (multipletests(..., method="holm")), and label scanned results as exploratory.
  • Every test assumes independent observations. SciPy does not skip missing values: .dropna() first.
  • Report template: what was compared (and n) · the estimate · its 95% CI · the test and p · the effect size in words · caveats.
Worked example

Do Bend and Flagstaff shoppers differ in how likely they are to recommend Pinecrest? Hypotheses first, then the test, the interval and a careful sentence.

import pandas as pd
from scipy import stats

survey = pd.read_csv("data/survey.csv")
bend = survey.loc[survey["store"] == "Bend", "recommend"].dropna()
flag = survey.loc[survey["store"] == "Flagstaff", "recommend"].dropna()
# H0: Bend and Flagstaff shoppers have the same mean recommend score.
# H1: the means differ (two-sided). alpha = 0.05. Welch t-test.

res = stats.ttest_ind(bend, flag, equal_var=False)
ci = res.confidence_interval()
print(f"Bend n = {len(bend)}, mean {bend.mean():.2f}; "
      f"Flagstaff n = {len(flag)}, mean {flag.mean():.2f}")
print(f"difference {bend.mean() - flag.mean():.2f}, "
      f"95% CI {ci.low:.2f} to {ci.high:.2f}")
print(f"t = {res.statistic:.2f}, p = {res.pvalue:.2f}")
Bend n = 223, mean 7.27; Flagstaff n = 207, mean 7.39
difference -0.11, 95% CI -0.51 to 0.28
t = -0.57, p = 0.57

p = 0.57: a gap this size (about a tenth of a point) turns up by chance more than half the time, so you fail to reject the null. The careful sentence: "We did not detect a difference between Bend and Flagstaff shoppers; the data are consistent with Bend being anywhere from about half a point lower to about a quarter of a point higher." It does not say the two stores are the same.

Glossary

  • Scatter plot: one point per row, one variable on each axis; read direction, form and strength from it.
  • Pearson's r: a number from -1 to +1 for straight-line association: the average product of z-scores.
  • Spearman's rho: Pearson's r on the ranks: consistent (monotonic) association, robust to outliers, suited to ordinal data.
  • Monotonic: consistently rising or consistently falling, whether or not along a straight line.
  • Correlation matrix: a square table of the correlation of every pair of columns.
  • Confounder: a third variable that influences both variables being correlated.
  • Partial correlation: the correlation of two variables after removing the straight-line effect of a third from each.
  • Simpson's paradox: a pooled correlation with the opposite sign to the correlation inside every group.
  • Regression line: the least-squares line y = intercept + slope x; predictor first in stats.linregress(x, y).
  • r squared: the share of the variance in y that the straight line accounts for.
  • Residual: actual value minus the value the line predicts.
  • Null hypothesis (H0): the "nothing is going on" claim assumed in order to test it.
  • Alternative hypothesis (H1): what you conclude if the null does not hold up; usually two-sided.
  • p-value: the probability, if the null hypothesis were true, of a result at least as extreme as the one observed.
  • Significance level (alpha): the cutoff chosen before looking, usually 0.05.
  • Permutation test: a test that builds the null by shuffling group labels many times.
  • Welch's t-test: a two-sample t-test that does not assume equal variances (equal_var=False).
  • Paired t-test: a test on the differences within matched pairs (ttest_rel).
  • Chi-square test: a test of independence for a table of counts (chi2_contingency).
  • Mann-Whitney U: a rank-based test for two independent groups; effect size U / (n1 x n2).
  • Effect size: how big a difference is: Cohen's d, a rate ratio, Cramér's V, a probability of superiority.
  • Type I / Type II error: a false alarm (rate alpha) / a miss.
  • Power: the probability that a test detects an effect that truly exists; it grows with sample size.
  • Multiple comparisons: running many tests, which multiplies false alarms; correct with Holm.

Session Goals

Today closes Module 4. By the end of the session you will have:

  • followed one real experiment, Pinecrest's new checkout page, from the plan written before launch to the decision to ship;
  • sized a test, checked that its randomization worked, and reported a difference with its confidence interval;
  • seen why looking early and slicing by segment both need care, and learned the word that keeps you honest: exploratory;
  • run a seven-step routine on a dataset from first question to written findings, and used it to work a business rule out of raw orders;
  • done Checkpoint 1: thirty minutes on your own with a small question. It is open notebook, it is not graded, and it is the best practice for the capstone you will get.
How today is different

Two lessons and a checkpoint fit in three hours only because the room does the core of each lesson and you finish the projects at home. That is normal for this session. The homework checklist at the end tells you what to do first.

Lessons

4.6

A/B Testing

Key ideas
  • Why experiment? In observational data the groups you compare differ in more ways than the one you care about. Random assignment makes the groups alike in everything except the treatment, including things nobody measured. That is what lets you use the word "caused".
  • Plan before data. Six lines, written first: the unit (what is randomized and counted), the primary metric (the one number that decides), the guardrail (what must not get worse), the hypotheses (two-sided, alpha 0.05), the sample size and duration, and the decision rule.
  • Sample size comes from four ingredients: the baseline rate, the smallest lift worth catching, alpha and power. proportion_effectsize(target, baseline) turns the two rates into Cohen's h; NormalIndPower().solve_power(...) finds whichever one of effect size, sample size, alpha and power you leave out. Small lifts on low rates need many visitors: 8.9% to 10.0% needs 11,092 per arm.
  • Check the experiment before the result: one row per unit; a split consistent with the design (stats.binomtest against 50/50); arms balanced on things fixed in advance. A failed check means stop and find the bug, not adjust.
  • Report four things: the difference in percentage points, its 95% confidence interval, the relative lift (and say which is which), and the p-value. Then lay the interval next to what is worth having.
  • Peeking (stopping the first time p dips below 0.05) is multiple testing in disguise: false alarms rise from 5% to about 12% with weekly looks and past 25% with daily looks. Fix the sample size and decide once.
  • Segments are exploratory. "Significant here, not there" does not show that here and there differ. Test the difference between the two lifts, correct for the slices, and label unplanned results.
  • Judge on every randomized visitor. Buyers are not a randomized group, because the page helps decide who buys. Revenue per visitor (a non-buyer counts as $0) keeps the protection of the coin flip.
The plan in six lines (fill in for the checkout test as you watch the demo)
LineFor the checkout test
Unit
Primary metric
Guardrail
Hypotheses and alpha
Sample size and duration
Decision rule
Worked example: does the new page work better on weekends?

A colleague says weekend shoppers are in less of a hurry, so the new page "must matter more on weekends". Check it the way the lesson checks devices: compare each slice, then test the difference between the lifts. (ab and compare_rates are the lesson's.)

ab["weekend"] = ab["date"].dt.dayofweek >= 5
weekday = compare_rates(ab[~ab["weekend"]])
weekend = compare_rates(ab[ab["weekend"]])
for label, r in [("Mon-Fri", weekday), ("Sat-Sun", weekend)]:
    print(f"{label}: {r['visitors']:>6,} visitors  "
          f"{r['control']:.2%} -> {r['treatment']:.2%}  {r['diff'] * 100:+.2f} pts "
          f"(95% CI {r['ci_low'] * 100:+.2f} to {r['ci_high'] * 100:+.2f})  "
          f"p = {r['p']:.3f}")

gap = weekend["diff"] - weekday["diff"]
gap_se = np.sqrt(weekend["se"] ** 2 + weekday["se"] ** 2)
p_gap = 2 * stats.norm.sf(abs(gap / gap_se))
print(f"Weekend lift minus weekday lift: {gap * 100:+.2f} points, p = {p_gap:.2f}")
Mon-Fri: 20,082 visitors  8.88% -> 9.91%  +1.03 pts (95% CI +0.22 to +1.84)  p = 0.012
Sat-Sun:  7,918 visitors  8.80% -> 10.33%  +1.53 pts (95% CI +0.23 to +2.83)  p = 0.021
Weekend lift minus weekday lift: +0.50 points, p = 0.52

Reading it. Both slices show a lift, and both are significant by themselves. The weekend lift looks larger (+1.53 against +1.03 points), but the difference between them is half a point with p = 0.52: no evidence that the page works differently on weekends. Notice the weekend interval is wider. There are fewer weekend visitors, so the estimate is rougher. Nobody planned this comparison, so the sentence for a memo is: "The lift is similar on weekdays and weekends (exploratory)."

P2
The lesson needed 11,092 visitors per arm for 80% power. This asks for 90% power. Will the number be about 12,000, about 15,000 or about 22,000?
h = proportion_effectsize(0.100, 0.089)
n90 = NormalIndPower().solve_power(effect_size=h, alpha=0.05, power=0.90)
print(round(n90))
P3
Two splits out of 10,000 visitors: 5,100 / 4,900 and 5,080 / 4,920. Which of them, if either, fails the 50/50 check at 0.05?
print(round(stats.binomtest(5100, 10_000, p=0.5).pvalue, 3))
print(round(stats.binomtest(5080, 10_000, p=0.5).pvalue, 3))

Q1
Pinecrest showed the old and the new checkout page during the same four weeks and let a coin flip decide who saw which. What does the coin flip buy?
A) Two groups of exactly the same size, which the z-test requires
B) A smaller sample size than a before/after comparison would need
C) Groups that are alike in everything except the page, including things nobody measured
D) A guarantee that the difference between the pages is not due to chance
Q2
A colleague sizes a test for a rise from 8.9% to 10.0% like this and gets 129,733 visitors per arm. What went wrong?
n = NormalIndPower().solve_power(effect_size=0.011, alpha=0.05, power=0.80)
A) effect_size expects Cohen's h from proportion_effectsize(0.100, 0.089), not the raw difference in rates
B) power should be 0.20, the chance of missing the effect
C) solve_power needs nobs1 as well, or it has nothing to solve
D) Nothing: a one-point lift really needs about 130,000 visitors per arm
Q3
Conversion went from 8.86% to 10.03%. What does this print? (First number: percentage points. Second: relative lift.)
print(round((0.1003 - 0.0886) * 100, 2), round(0.1003 / 0.0886 - 1, 3))
Q4
An experiment planned as 50/50 reports 14,400 visitors in treatment and 13,600 in control. stats.binomtest(14400, 28000, p=0.5).pvalue is 1.8e-06. What do you do?
A) Carry on: 51.4% is close enough to half
B) Drop 800 treatment visitors at random so the arms match, then analyze
C) Report the conversion result with a note that the split was uneven
D) Stop and find where the control visitors went: the experiment did not run as planned, so its results cannot be read
Q5

A) About 5%, because alpha is 0.05 at each look
B) About 12%, because each look is another chance to cross the line by luck
C) About 1.25%, because the four looks share the 5%
D) About 50%, because the p-value wanders at random
Q6

A) The new page works on mobile and does nothing on desktop
B) The new page should be shipped to mobile visitors only
C) The gain appears concentrated on mobile; the evidence that devices differ is borderline and exploratory, and worth a follow-up test
D) The desktop result proves the page has no effect on desktop
4.7

An EDA Workflow

Key ideas
  • EDA is a routine, not "make charts until something looks interesting": 1 Questions · 2 Structure · 3 Quality · 4 One variable · 5 Two variables · 6 Segments · 7 Findings and caveats, and then around again with the new questions.
  • Questions come first. They decide which columns matter, which comparisons are planned and which are fishing, and when you are done. Changing a question openly is fine; inventing one afterward to fit a pretty chart is not.
  • Know the grain: what one row is, and which column identifies it. Every count and every average depends on it. Make merges prove the relationship you expect with validate=.
  • For missing data ask "missing for whom?" Values that are not missing at random change who a column describes. Decide per question; dropna(subset=[...]), never a blanket dropna().
  • Ask who is in the data compared with who you want to talk about. The survey speaks for active, fairly loyal customers.
  • Look before you summarize: every distribution before its mean, the grouped values behind every correlation.
  • Scanning segments is multiple testing. Two disciplines: (1) count every comparison and correct (Holm); (2) for what survives, look at the size of the gap and check the obvious competing explanation.
  • An analysis ends in writing: findings (one sentence with a number each), caveats (who and what the data cannot speak for), next questions.
The seven steps (write one line each on what you would actually do)
StepWhat I do
1 Questions
2 Structure
3 Quality
4 One variable
5 Two variables
6 Segments
7 Findings and caveats
Worked example: do frequent buyers recommend Pinecrest more?

A new question for the survey, and a merge that would go wrong if you were careless. orders has one row per order, the survey one row per customer. Merging them directly would repeat each response once per order (1,800 rows become 9,230). So summarize the orders to one row per customer first, then merge, and let validate prove it. (survey is the lesson's.)

orders = pd.read_csv("data/orders.csv")
per_customer = (orders.groupby("customer_id")
                      .agg(n_orders=("order_id", "count"))
                      .reset_index())
print("orders:", len(orders), "rows -> one row per customer:", len(per_customer))

joined = survey.merge(per_customer, on="customer_id", how="left",
                      validate="one_to_one")
print("survey rows before:", len(survey), "after:", len(joined),
      "| no orders found:", joined["n_orders"].isna().sum())

rho = joined["n_orders"].corr(joined["recommend"], method="spearman")
print(f"Spearman correlation, number of orders and recommend score: {rho:.2f}")
joined["buyer"] = pd.cut(joined["n_orders"], [0, 2, 5, 10, 100],
                         labels=["1-2", "3-5", "6-10", "11+"])
by_buyer = joined.groupby("buyer", observed=True)["recommend"]
print(by_buyer.agg(["count", "median", "mean"]).round(2))
orders: 20302 rows -> one row per customer: 3942
survey rows before: 1800 after: 1800 | no orders found: 0
Spearman correlation, number of orders and recommend score: -0.02
       count  median  mean
buyer                     
1-2      559     8.0  7.32
3-5      572     8.0  7.48
6-10     414     7.0  7.30
11+      201     7.0  7.23

Reading it. The merge kept 1,800 rows and every respondent has orders (step 3 of the routine already said so). The correlation is -0.02: no relationship. The grouped values agree: the mean recommend score is between 7.2 and 7.5 whether someone has ordered twice or a dozen times. An honest finding: "Among survey respondents, how often a customer orders is unrelated to how likely they are to recommend Pinecrest (Spearman -0.02)." A caveat: respondents are all active customers, so this says nothing about people who stopped buying. A null result, checked and written down, is a result.

Checkpoint 1: analysis task on Modules 1 to 4

The question

Pinecrest's store buyers believe that December shoppers buy more expensive things. Finance wants a number before it plans next December's stock. Using data/daily_sales.csv: is the average price per unit sold (revenue ÷ units) higher in December than in the rest of the year? By how much, how sure are you, and is it the same in all four stores?

daily_sales.csv has one row per store per day: date, store, visitors, transactions, units, revenue, staff_on_duty, promo_day.

What to hand in (one notebook or .py file)
  1. A plan, written before any code (three lines): what you will compute for each row, which rows you will compare with which, and what you will check before trusting the numbers. About 3 minutes.
  2. Your checks: what one row is, what is missing, what you did about it. About 5 minutes.
  3. The comparison: the difference in dollars, a 95% confidence interval, a suitable test and an effect size. Then: is it the same in all four stores? About 10 minutes.
  4. One figure with a title that states your finding, and labeled axes. About 5 minutes.
  5. A note: two findings with numbers, two caveats that are specific to this data, one next question. About 7 minutes. Start it by minute 25 whatever happens.

Glossary

A/B test: an experiment that shows two versions to randomly assigned groups at the same time and compares one planned metric. Arm: one of the groups in an experiment (control, treatment). Unit: what is randomized and counted: here, one visitor. Primary metric: the single number, chosen in advance, that decides the test. Guardrail metric: a number that must not get worse while the primary metric improves. Minimum detectable effect: the smallest improvement the test is sized to have a good chance of catching. Power: the chance of detecting an effect of a given size if it is real (0.80 by convention). Cohen's h: the effect size for a difference between two proportions; what statsmodels power functions expect. Sample ratio mismatch: a split between arms that differs from the design by more than chance allows; a sign of a bug. Percentage points: the absolute difference between two rates (10.03% - 8.86% = 1.17 points). Relative lift: the difference as a share of the baseline (1.17 / 8.86 = 13.2%). Peeking: checking a running test and stopping when it first looks significant; inflates false alarms. Exploratory: found by looking, not planned in advance; a lead to confirm, not a confirmed result. EDA: exploratory data analysis: getting to know a dataset before relying on it. Grain: what one row of a table represents. Key: the column (or columns) whose values identify a row. Missing not at random: gaps that are more common for some kinds of rows, so the column describes only those who answered. Holm correction: an adjustment to p-values that accounts for having run several tests. Caveat: a specific statement of who or what the data cannot speak for.

SQLWhat it doespandas
SELECT a, bChoose columnsdf[["a", "b"]]
WHERE x > 5Keep rowsdf[df["x"] > 5]
GROUP BY g with aggregatesOne row per groupdf.groupby("g").agg(...)

LEFT JOIN ... WHERE b.key IS NULLRows of A with no partnerindicator=True, keep left_only

Lessons

5.1

Database Connections

Key ideas
  • A database answers questions and returns only what you ask for. SQL is the language of the request. SQLite is a complete database engine in one file; Python includes it as sqlite3.
  • Work on a copy (shutil.copy) or open the file read-only, so the original stays as documented.
  • Connection, cursor, rows. sqlite3.connect() gives a connection. Running a statement gives a cursor that walks forward through the result. Rows come back as tuples; fetchone(), fetchmany(n), fetchall().
  • One statement does most of the work: SELECT ... FROM ... WHERE ... GROUP BY ... HAVING ... ORDER BY ... LIMIT, always written in that order. The engine runs it starting from FROM: FROM, WHERE, GROUP BY, HAVING, SELECT, ORDER BY, LIMIT.
  • WHERE filters rows before grouping; HAVING filters groups after it. Every selected column is either in the GROUP BY or inside an aggregate.
  • Three COUNTs: COUNT(*) rows, COUNT(col) rows where col is not NULL, COUNT(DISTINCT col) different values.
  • Values go in through placeholders (? with a tuple, or :name with a dictionary). Never with an f-string, .format(), % or +. A one-item tuple needs its comma: ("Boise",).
  • Changes are saved only on commit. with conn: commits if the block finishes and rolls back if it raises: all or nothing.
  • SQLAlchemy gives one way to talk to any database: create_engine(url), with engine.connect() as c:, text("...") with :name parameters.
  • pd.read_sql(query, connection, params=...) brings a result into a DataFrame; df.to_sql(name, engine, if_exists=..., index=False) saves one as a table. Let the database do the heavy filtering and grouping.
Worked example: a report function with parameters

Marketing asks, for any year: how many completed orders used each promo code, and what was the average order? The year changes, so it travels through params. (The f-strings build the two date VALUES from an integer; the SQL text itself is fixed. conn is the lesson's connection.)

def promo_report(conn, year):
    """Orders and average total for each promo code in one calendar year."""
    sql = """
        SELECT promo_code,
               MAX(discount_pct)          AS discount_pct,
               COUNT(*)                   AS orders,
               ROUND(AVG(order_total), 2) AS avg_total
        FROM orders
        WHERE status = 'completed'
          AND order_date >= :start AND order_date < :end
        GROUP BY promo_code
        HAVING COUNT(*) >= 100
        ORDER BY orders DESC
    """
    params = {"start": f"{year}-01-01", "end": f"{year + 1}-01-01"}
    return pd.read_sql(sql, conn, params=params)

print(promo_report(conn, 2025))
  promo_code  discount_pct  orders  avg_total
0        NaN             0    9162     197.15
1  WELCOME10            10     262     183.36
2    GEARUP5             5     235     159.03
3    TRAIL15            15     183     170.37
4   SUMMIT20            20     128     141.76

Reading it. The first row has no promo code: NULL is its own group, and pandas shows it as NaN. Most orders use no code. HAVING COUNT(*) >= 100 removed the smaller groups after grouping. The averages fall as the discount rises (SUMMIT20 orders average $141.76), partly because order_total is the amount AFTER the discount: compare like with like before you conclude anything about basket size. Call promo_report(conn, 2024) and the same function answers for another year, with no SQL rewritten.

Notes
Self-check (do these at home, without notes first)
Q1
A query is written SELECT ... FROM ... WHERE ... GROUP BY ... HAVING ... ORDER BY ... LIMIT. Which clause does the engine work on first?
A) SELECT, because it is written first
B) WHERE, because rows must be filtered before anything else
C) FROM, because the engine has to pick the table before it can filter, group or select
D) ORDER BY, because sorted rows are faster to filter
Q2
What does this print?
conn = sqlite3.connect(":memory:")
conn.execute("CREATE TABLE t (x INTEGER, y TEXT)")
conn.executemany("INSERT INTO t VALUES (?, ?)",
                 [(1, "a"), (2, None), (3, "c")])
print(conn.execute("SELECT COUNT(*), COUNT(y), SUM(x) FROM t").fetchone())
Q3

A) Pass a one-item tuple: ("Boise",)
B) Write five placeholders: ?????
C) Put the value in the text with an f-string
D) Use double quotes around the ?

A) status = 'completed'
B) AVG(order_total) > 190
C) order_date >= '2025-01-01'

A) conn.execute(f"SELECT * FROM orders WHERE store = '{store}'")
B) conn.execute("SELECT * FROM orders WHERE store = '" + store + "'")

Q6
What does this print?
conn = sqlite3.connect(":memory:")
conn.execute("CREATE TABLE t (k INTEGER PRIMARY KEY)")
conn.commit()
try:
    with conn:
        conn.execute("INSERT INTO t VALUES (1)")
        conn.execute("INSERT INTO t VALUES (1)")
except sqlite3.IntegrityError:
    pass
print(conn.execute("SELECT COUNT(*) FROM t").fetchone()[0])
5.2

Complex Queries

Key ideas
  • A join pairs rows by key. Join types differ only in what happens to a row with no partner.
  • JOIN (inner) keeps matched pairs only and drops the rest without telling you. LEFT JOIN keeps every row of the first table, with NULL where there is no partner.
  • The anti-join answers "which ones are missing?": LEFT JOIN ... WHERE right.key IS NULL. NOT EXISTS does the same job.
  • Count after every join. Four numbers to remember: 739 orders have no customer row; 1,058 customers never ordered; 609 order lines have no product row; 8 products never sold.
  • The fan-out trap: joining to a "many" side repeats the "one" side. Sum a column at the level where it lives; when you need two levels, aggregate first (a subquery or CTE), then join.
  • Subqueries return a value, a list or a table depending on where they sit. CTEs (WITH name AS (...)) give the steps names, so a long query reads top to bottom.
  • A recursive CTE (anchor, UNION ALL, recursive part) walks a hierarchy of any depth. It stops when a round finds no new rows.
  • CASE is if / elif / else. Inside SUM or COUNT it makes side-by-side columns. Multiply by 100.0, not 100, or integer division gives you zeros.
  • NULL means unknown. = NULL never matches (use IS NULL); a condition and its opposite do not add up to everything; NOT IN with a NULL in the list returns nothing. COALESCE(x, y) is the SQL fillna.
How the four tables connect (finish the sketch you brought)
LinkKeyWhich side is "one"?Unmatched rows
customers and orderscustomer_id
orders and order_itemsorder_id
products and order_itemssku
Worked example: return rate by loyalty tier, with every order accounted for

Does the return rate differ by the customer's loyalty tier? The tier is in customers, the status in orders. An inner join would quietly leave out the 739 orders from closed accounts, so this uses a LEFT JOIN and names those rows with COALESCE. (conn and show are the lesson's.)

show("""
    SELECT COALESCE(c.loyalty_tier, '(closed account)') AS tier,
           COUNT(*)                                     AS orders,
           SUM(CASE WHEN o.status = 'returned' THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS returned,
           ROUND(100.0 * SUM(CASE WHEN o.status = 'returned' THEN 1 ELSE 0 END)
                 / COUNT(*), 1)                         AS return_pct
    FROM orders AS o
    LEFT JOIN customers AS c ON c.customer_id = o.customer_id
    GROUP BY tier
    ORDER BY orders DESC
""")
show("""
    SELECT COUNT(*) AS rows_with_inner_join
    FROM orders AS o
    JOIN customers AS c ON c.customer_id = o.customer_id
""")
            tier  orders  returned  return_pct
            Gold    8491       403         4.7
          Silver    7132       383         5.4
           Basic    3940       316         8.0
(closed account)     739        41         5.5
 rows_with_inner_join
                19563

Reading it. The orders column adds up to 20,302: every order is counted once. The second query shows what an inner join would have kept: 19,563. Basic customers' orders come back 8.0% of the time against 4.7% for Gold. Before calling that a finding, ask how the tier is made: it is earned from COMPLETED spend, and a returned order does not count. A customer who returns a lot is held in a lower tier, so the tier partly reflects returns rather than causing them. SQL got the numbers right; the reading needs Lesson 4.4's question: how was this data generated? The pattern to keep: LEFT JOIN from the table you care about, COALESCE to name the unmatched, conditional aggregation for the rate, and a row-count check at the end.

Glossary

SQL: the language for asking a relational database for data. SQLite: a complete SQL database engine that lives in one file; included in Python as sqlite3. Connection: an open line from your program to a database. Cursor: a pointer that runs one statement and walks forward through its result. Aggregate function: a function that turns many rows into one value: COUNT, SUM, AVG, MIN, MAX. GROUP BY: split rows into groups and return one row per group. HAVING: a filter on groups, applied after GROUP BY. Placeholder: a ? or :name in the SQL text where a value will be supplied separately. SQL injection: an attack in which text supplied as a value becomes part of the SQL command. Transaction: a batch of changes that is saved all together (commit) or not at all (rollback). Engine (SQLAlchemy): an object made from a database URL that hands out connections. Key: a column whose value identifies a row, or points at a row in another table. Inner join: keeps only rows that found a partner. Left join: keeps every row of the first table; unmatched rows get NULL on the other side. Anti-join: the rows of one table that have no partner in another. Fan-out: the repetition of "one"-side rows when joined to a "many" side; it inflates sums. Subquery: a SELECT in parentheses inside another query. CTE: common table expression: a named query defined with WITH and used like a table in the statement that follows. Recursive CTE: a CTE that refers to itself to walk a hierarchy level by level. CASE: SQL's if / elif / else expression. NULL: the marker for an unknown or missing value; comparisons with it are unknown. COALESCE: returns its first argument that is not NULL.
