Session 15: SQL Windows and Query Speed

Your notes, worked examples, labs and self-check for window functions and query speed.

Lessons 5.3–5.4 Session 15 · 3 hours Name: ____________________

Session Goals

Today finishes the SQL half of Module 5. By the end of the session you will have:

  • put group averages, shares, ranks, running totals and moving averages on every row, without losing the rows;
  • picked the top N rows of every group with the three-step recipe;
  • compared each row with the one before it, within its own series;
  • read the engine's plan for a query, added an index, and shown its effect on the plan and on a fair timing;
  • seen the cases where an index cannot help, and replaced a loop of queries with one;
  • checked every answer by a second route: pandas, or a number you already knew.
Three questions for every window

Partition: which rows belong together? Order: in what order, and is it unique? Frame: how many of those rows does the function look at? Say them before you type the OVER.

Lessons

5.3

SQL Window Functions

Key ideas
  • A window function computes over a set of related rows and keeps every row; GROUP BY collapses them. Any aggregate becomes a window function with OVER (...).
  • OVER (PARTITION BY ... ORDER BY ... ROWS BETWEEN ...): which rows belong together, in what order, and how many of them to look at. An empty OVER () is one window holding every row.
  • ROW_NUMBER, RANK, DENSE_RANK differ only in ties: 1, 2, 3, 4 · 1, 1, 3, 4 · 1, 1, 2, 3.
  • Top N per group: number the rows in a CTE, then filter outside it. WHERE runs before window functions exist.
  • LAG and LEAD read the previous and next row. Partition by the series, or one series reads another's rows.
  • Running totals and moving averages need an explicit ROWS frame. The default frame treats rows that tie in the order as one.
  • SQL uses partial frames at the start of a series; pandas rolling gives NaN there unless min_periods=1. A frame ending at 1 PRECEDING never sees the current row.
  • End every window's ORDER BY with a unique column so results are the same every run.
Window functions and their pandas twins
NeedSQLpandas
Group value on every rowAVG(x) OVER (PARTITION BY g)groupby("g")["x"].transform("mean")
Number or rank rowsROW_NUMBER(), RANK(), DENSE_RANK()rank(method="first" / "min" / "dense")
Previous or next rowLAG(x), LEAD(x)shift(1), shift(-1)
Running totalSUM(x) OVER (ORDER BY t ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW)cumsum()
Moving averageAVG(x) OVER (ORDER BY t ROWS BETWEEN 2 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW)rolling(3, min_periods=1).mean()
Earlier rows only (no leakage)... ROWS BETWEEN 3 PRECEDING AND 1 PRECEDINGshift(1).rolling(3, min_periods=1)
Equal-sized bucketsNTILE(4) OVER (ORDER BY x)pd.qcut(x, 4)
Share of groupx / SUM(x) OVER (PARTITION BY g)x / groupby("g")["x"].transform("sum")
Worked example: each store's weakest month, and how weak it was

Store managers ask: "Which month was our worst, and how far below our own average was it?" That is a bottom-1 per group (ROW_NUMBER ordered by revenue ascending) and a group average on the same row. (conn and show are the lesson's read-only connection and helper.)

show("""
    WITH store_month AS (
        SELECT store, strftime('%Y-%m', order_date) AS month,
               ROUND(SUM(order_total), 2) AS revenue
        FROM orders
        WHERE status = 'completed' AND store <> 'Online'
        GROUP BY store, month
    ),
    ranked AS (
        SELECT store, month, revenue,
               ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY store ORDER BY revenue, month) AS rn,
               ROUND(AVG(revenue) OVER (PARTITION BY store), 2) AS store_avg
        FROM store_month
    )
    SELECT store, month AS weakest_month, revenue, store_avg,
           ROUND(100.0 * revenue / store_avg, 1) AS pct_of_avg
    FROM ranked
    WHERE rn = 1
    ORDER BY store
""")
    store weakest_month  revenue  store_avg  pct_of_avg
Asheville       2025-07  5754.39    9907.47        58.1
     Bend       2025-02  6874.57   11857.30        58.0
    Boise       2024-01 11237.18   18474.45        60.8
Flagstaff       2024-01  6660.61   12524.18        53.2

Reading it. Each store's weakest month ran at 53% to 61% of that store's own monthly average. Three of the four are in January or February; Asheville's is July 2025. Ordering by revenue, month makes the choice unique even if two months tied. The average and the rank sit on the same row because both are windows over the same partition; with GROUP BY you would have needed two queries and a join.

P1
24 months. How many have a value twelve months earlier?
show(MONTHLY + """
    , lagged AS (
        SELECT month, LAG(revenue, 12) OVER (ORDER BY month) AS year_before
        FROM monthly
    )
    SELECT COUNT(*) AS months, COUNT(year_before) AS with_a_year_before
    FROM lagged
""")
P2
Seven rows into three buckets. Which bucket gets the extra row?
show("""
    SELECT NTILE(3) OVER (ORDER BY v) AS bucket, v
    FROM (SELECT 1 AS v UNION ALL SELECT 2 UNION ALL SELECT 3 UNION ALL SELECT 4
          UNION ALL SELECT 5 UNION ALL SELECT 6 UNION ALL SELECT 7)
""")
P3
LAST_VALUE with the default frame. What is in the last_value column for the first two months?
show(MONTHLY + """
    SELECT month, revenue,
           LAST_VALUE(revenue) OVER (ORDER BY month) AS last_value
    FROM monthly
    ORDER BY month
    LIMIT 2
""")

A) On every row: SQL counts rows, pandas counts days
B) Nowhere: the two are identical
C) Only on the last row, because SQL has no following rows

5.4

Query Optimization

Key ideas
  • SQL is declarative: you say what you want; the planner chooses how. EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN shows its choice without running the query.
  • SCAN reads every row; SEARCH jumps. A scan is fine when you need most of the table, and a problem when you wanted a few rows.
  • An index is a sorted copy of some columns plus a pointer to each row. Primary keys are indexed automatically; foreign keys and filter columns usually are not.
  • Joins run as nested loops. The inner step runs once per outer row, so it should be a SEARCH. The planner chooses the table order, not your FROM clause.
  • Composite indexes follow the leftmost-prefix rule: (a, b) serves a, or a and b, but not b alone. Equality columns first, the range or sort last. A covering index answers without opening the table.
  • An index cannot help when the column is inside a function or arithmetic, a pattern starts with %, most rows match, or the table is tiny.
  • ANALYZE gives the planner statistics. Every index costs write speed and file size.
  • Measure properly: best of several runs, plan before and after, identical results. Work on a copy; open it with cached_statements=0 when you drop indexes and re-plan.
  • Ask for less: name your columns, aggregate in SQL, and send one query instead of a loop of queries.
Reading a plan
The plan saysIt meansCost grows with
SCAN tRead every row and test each onethe size of the table
SEARCH t USING INTEGER PRIMARY KEY (rowid=?)Jump straight to the rowalmost nothing
SEARCH t USING INDEX i (col=?)Find entries in the index, then fetch those rowsthe number of matching rows
SEARCH t USING COVERING INDEX i (...)The index alone answers; the table is not touchedthe number of matching rows
SCAN t USING (COVERING) INDEX iStill reads everything, but through the indexthe size of the table
USE TEMP B-TREE FOR ORDER BYA separate sorting stepthe number of rows sorted
Worked example: should the product lookup get an index?

The shop's search box filters products by brand and price. One composite index, the plan before and after, and a fair timing. NOT INDEXED tells SQLite to ignore indexes, so the "without" timing runs on the same connection. (conn, plan and timed are the lesson's, on the working copy.)

LOOKUP = "SELECT name, price FROM products WHERE brand = ? AND price < ?"
plan(LOOKUP, ("Tallgrass", 50))
conn.execute("CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS idx_products_brand_price "
             "ON products (brand, price)")
conn.commit()
plan(LOOKUP, ("Tallgrass", 50))
print("rows:", len(conn.execute(LOOKUP, ("Tallgrass", 50)).fetchall()), "of",
      conn.execute("SELECT COUNT(*) FROM products").fetchone()[0], "products")
NO_INDEX = "SELECT name, price FROM products NOT INDEXED WHERE brand = ? AND price < ?"
t_without = timed(NO_INDEX, [("Tallgrass", 50)] * 500)
t_with = timed(LOOKUP, [("Tallgrass", 50)] * 500)
print(f"500 lookups: {t_without:.1f} ms without the index, {t_with:.1f} ms with it")
conn.execute("DROP INDEX idx_products_brand_price")
conn.commit()
    SCAN products
    SEARCH products USING INDEX idx_products_brand_price (brand=? AND price<?)
rows: 11 of 242 products
500 lookups: 7.4 ms without the index, 4.5 ms with it

Reading it. The plan changed from SCAN to SEARCH, and 500 lookups got a few milliseconds faster in total (your numbers will differ). With 242 products the whole table fits in a few pages: scanning it is nearly as fast as any index, and a few milliseconds over 500 searches is nothing a shop page would notice. This is the fourth case in "When an Index Does Not Help": a small table. The honest recommendation is no index, and the cell drops it again.

Glossary

Window function: a function computed over a set of rows related to the current row, returning one value per row. Partition: the group of rows a window function looks within (PARTITION BY). Frame: the part of the partition the function actually uses for the current row (ROWS BETWEEN ... AND ...). ROW_NUMBER / RANK / DENSE_RANK: numbering that never repeats / repeats on ties and skips / repeats on ties without skipping. LAG / LEAD: the value from the previous / next row of the window. Running total: a sum from the first row of the partition to the current row. Moving average: an average over a frame that slides with the current row. NTILE: deals ordered rows into a number of nearly equal buckets. Query planner: the part of the engine that chooses how to run a query. EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN: returns the planner's chosen strategy instead of running the query. SCAN / SEARCH: read every row / jump to matching rows using an index or the primary key. Index: a separate sorted structure of one or more columns that lets the engine find rows without reading the whole table. Composite index: an index on several columns, usable from the leftmost column onward. Covering index: an index that contains every column a query needs, so the table is not read. Selectivity: how small a share of the rows a filter keeps; indexes pay off on selective filters. ANALYZE: gathers statistics about tables and indexes for the planner. N+1 problem: running one query for a list and then one more query per item, instead of one combined query.

Two halves, one idea

Before the break the data is nested documents in a database. After the break it is nested documents arriving over the web. Both end where everything in this course ends: in a DataFrame you already know how to clean, summarize and plot.

Lessons

5.5

NoSQL with MongoDB

Key ideas
  • A document is one record that can hold nested objects and lists: one website visit with its device, its pages and its cart. A collection is a set of documents, and they need not all have the same fields.
  • A query is a dictionary: {"converted": True}. Several pairs mean AND. Reach inside nested objects with dot notation in quotes: "device.type".
  • Anything other than "equals" uses an operator that starts with $: {"cart.value": {"$gte": 500}}, $in, $exists, $regex.
  • Missing is not null. In a query, None matches both. {"$exists": False} asks precisely "is the field absent?"
  • A condition on a list field ("pages.path") is true when any element matches. Two conditions on the same element need $elemMatch.
  • The aggregation pipeline is a list of stages. $match filters (WHERE, or HAVING after a group), $group summarizes (GROUP BY), $unwind makes one document per list element (pandas explode), $sort and $limit order and cut.
  • Inside a pipeline, a field's value is written with a leading dollar: "$device.type". In a query, the field name is just the key, with no dollar.
  • A wrong field name does not raise an error. It returns zero. Be suspicious of zero: look at one document with find_one() first.
  • Finish in pandas: pd.DataFrame(list(cursor)) for flat results, pd.json_normalize(docs) for nested ones.
SQLMongoDB
TableCollection
RowDocument
ColumnField
WHERE a = 1 AND b > 2{"a": 1, "b": {"$gt": 2}}
SELECT a, bprojection {"a": 1, "b": 1, "_id": 0}
GROUP BY + COUNT(*)$group with {"$sum": 1}
ORDER BY x DESC LIMIT 3.sort("x", -1).limit(3)
join to a child tablean embedded list, or $unwind
Worked example: the search audience

Question: how do visitors who arrive from a search engine behave? Read each block, cover the output, and say what you expect before you look.

Step 1. Three counts with one base query. {**news, ...} copies the dictionary and adds a condition. "cart.items.0" is "element 0 of the list": if it exists, the cart is not empty.

with open("data/web_sessions.json", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    sessions = json.load(f)["sessions"]
web = mongomock.MongoClient()["shop"]["sessions"]
web.insert_many(sessions)

news = {"utm.source": "search"}
print("search sessions :", web.count_documents(news))
has_cart = {"cart.items.0": {"$exists": True}}
print("  with a cart   :", web.count_documents({**news, **has_cart}))
print("  that ordered  :", web.count_documents({**news, "converted": True}))
search sessions : 915
  with a cart   : 201
  that ordered  : 85

Step 2. The same audience, grouped in one pipeline. $ifNull supplies a label when the campaign is missing; $cond inside $sum counts only the sessions that converted. The percentage is finished in pandas.

rows = list(web.aggregate([
    {"$match": news},
    {"$group": {"_id": {"$ifNull": ["$utm.campaign", "(no campaign)"]},
                "sessions": {"$sum": 1},
                "orders": {"$sum": {"$cond": ["$converted", 1, 0]}},
                "avg_cart": {"$avg": "$cart.value"}}},
    {"$sort": {"sessions": -1}},
]))
table = pd.DataFrame(rows).rename(columns={"_id": "campaign"})
table = table[["campaign", "sessions", "orders", "avg_cart"]]
table["order_pct"] = (100 * table["orders"] / table["sessions"]).round(1)
print(table.round(2).to_string(index=False))
        campaign  sessions  orders  avg_cart  order_pct
   (no campaign)       601      52     56.88        8.7
fall_gear_search       314      33     47.37       10.5

Step 3. Check it a second way. Flatten the same documents and count in pandas. 601 + 314 = 915 and 52 + 33 = 85: the two routes agree. Notice which sessions have no campaign: the organic ones. Nobody ran a campaign for them.

docs = list(web.find(news, {"_id": 0, "session_id": 1, "converted": 1,
                            "utm": 1}))
flat = pd.json_normalize(docs)
print(flat.shape, list(flat.columns))
print(flat.groupby("utm.medium")["converted"].agg(["size", "sum"]).to_string())
print(flat["utm.campaign"].isna().sum(), "rows with no campaign")
(915, 5) ['session_id', 'converted', 'utm.source', 'utm.medium', 'utm.campaign']
            size  sum
utm.medium           
cpc          314   33
organic      601   52
601 rows with no campaign

6.1

HTTP with Requests

The six ground rules (every session of Module 6 starts here)
  1. Read robots.txt and obey it. It is the owner's published request to programs: where they may go and how fast.
  2. Read the terms of use. If they forbid automated collection, the answer is no, however easy the code would be.
  3. Prefer an API or a download when one exists. It is the front door.
  4. Go slowly and say who you are. Pause between requests; send a User-Agent with a way to contact you.
  5. Never go around a login, a paywall or a block. If you are asked to stop, stop.
  6. Do not collect personal data. Names, emails, profiles and photos of real people are protected by law in many places.
Key ideas
  • HTTP is request and response. Out: a method (GET to read), a URL, headers, maybe a body. Back: a status code, headers, a body.
  • Status codes by first digit: 2xx success, 3xx look elsewhere (handled for you), 4xx your request has a problem, 5xx the server has a problem.
  • A 404 or a 500 is not an exception until you call r.raise_for_status(). The pattern is: get → raise_for_status() → use the body.
  • Rules first. Fetch robots.txt, parse it with RobotFileParser, ask can_fetch() and crawl_delay().
  • Send a User-Agent that names your project and gives a contact address. Never pretend to be a browser.
  • r.content is bytes; r.text is those bytes decoded with r.encoding. Junk like â˜… means the wrong encoding: set r.encoding = "utf-8".
  • Pass query parameters as a dictionary (params=) and read JSON with r.json(). A list of flat dictionaries goes straight into pd.DataFrame.
  • Every request gets a timeout. Without one, requests waits forever.
  • A Session reuses the connection and remembers your headers (not your timeout). Save r.content to disk once; parse the saved copy as often as you like.
RangeMeaningWhat to do
2xxSuccessUse the body
3xxLook elsewhereNothing: requests follows redirects
4xxYour request has a problem (400, 401, 403, 404, 429)Fix the request (429: wait first)
5xxThe server has a problem (500, 503)Wait and try again later
Worked example: does this product exist?

Question: the shop has a page for SLP-1004. Does it have one for SLP-9004? Ask the website, then ask the API, and compare how each one says "no".

Step 1. Ask the website. The function checks the status before it touches the body. For a missing page it returns the status and None: it never tries to read a title out of an error page. (Setup: BASE_URL, HEADERS, urljoin and time as in the demo.)

def product_page(sku):
    """Return (status, title) for one product page; title None if missing."""
    url = urljoin(BASE_URL, f"products/{sku}.html")
    resp = requests.get(url, headers=HEADERS, timeout=10)
    try:
        resp.raise_for_status()
    except requests.exceptions.HTTPError:
        return resp.status_code, None
    resp.encoding = "utf-8"
    html = resp.text
    title = html[html.index("<title>") + 7 : html.index("</title>")]
    return resp.status_code, title

for sku in ["SLP-1004", "SLP-9004"]:
    print(sku, product_page(sku))
    time.sleep(1)
SLP-1004 (200, 'Larkspur Ridge Backcountry Sleeping Pad | Pinecrest Outfitters (practice site)')
SLP-9004 (404, None)

Step 2. Ask the API. Same question, one line of parsing. The API answers a missing product with status 404 and a JSON body that explains itself. (API = "http://127.0.0.1:8000"; start it with python data/mock_api.py.)

for sku in ["SLP-1004", "SLP-9004"]:
    r = requests.get(f"{API}/products/{sku}", headers=HEADERS, timeout=10)
    body = r.json()
    print(sku, r.status_code, body.get("name") or body["error"]["message"])
SLP-1004 200 Larkspur Ridge Backcountry Sleeping Pad
SLP-9004 404 No product with SKU 'SLP-9004'.

Both sources agree, and neither needed you to guess: the status code told the program what happened, and the message told you.

Glossary

Document: one record in MongoDB: like a Python dictionary, and it may hold nested objects and lists.

Collection: a set of documents (the counterpart of a table). Its documents need not share a shape.

Dot notation: "device.type": the name of a field inside a nested object, written in quotes.

Operator: a key that starts with $ ($gt, $in, $exists, $set) and says how to compare or change.

Projection: the second argument of find: which fields to return (1) or leave out (0).

Aggregation pipeline: a list of stages ($match, $unwind, $group, $sort...) that each transform the documents and pass them on.

mongomock: a library that imitates a MongoDB server inside Python, with pymongo's methods. Data lives in memory only.

HTTP: the request-and-response conversation every web page and API is built on.

Status code: the three-digit summary of a response: 2xx success, 3xx look elsewhere, 4xx your request, 5xx the server.

Header: a name-and-value note that travels with a request or a response (User-Agent, Content-Type).

robots.txt: a site's published request to programs: where they may go and how fast.

Query parameters: the ?name=value&name=value part of a URL. Pass them to requests as a dictionary (params=).

Timeout: the longest requests will wait to connect, and between bytes. Without one it waits forever.

Mojibake: junk such as â˜… that appears when bytes are decoded with the wrong encoding.

API: addresses that return data (usually JSON) for programs instead of pages for people.

The six ground rules (every Module 6 session starts here)
  1. Read robots.txt and obey it. It is the owner's published request to programs: where they may go and how fast.
  2. Read the terms of use. If they forbid automated collection, the answer is no, however easy the code would be.
  3. Prefer an API or a download when one exists. It is the front door.
  4. Go slowly and say who you are. Pause between requests; send a User-Agent with a way to contact you.

6.2

Parsing HTML with Beautiful Soup

Key ideas
  • Pass resp.content (bytes) to BeautifulSoup(..., "html.parser"): it reads the page's own charset.
  • find() / select_one() return one element or None. find_all() / select() return a list, maybe empty.
  • Choose labels, not positions: li.product-card p.price, not main > ul > li:nth-of-type(3).
  • Prefer data- attributes (data-price) to formatted text ($299.95). Everything from HTML is a string: convert it.
  • Find the repeated block, then write one function that turns one block into one dictionary.
  • Missing attribute: tag["x"] raises KeyError, tag.get("x") gives None. Missing element: select_one gives None. Decide per field what missing should mean.
  • Follow the "next" link until there is none, pause between requests, and keep a page limit.
  • Check before you trust: the page's count, unique keys, plausible values.
  • pd.read_html(io.BytesIO(resp.content)) reads real tables; pick one with attrs={"id": ...}.
SelectorMatches
pevery p element
.priceclass includes price
#price-listthe element with that id
[data-sku]has the attribute
[data-sku="PCK-1003"]attribute has that value
A BB anywhere inside A
A > BB directly inside A
Worked example: the climbing gear that is running out

Question: which climbing products are low on stock or out of stock? The stock label is deep inside each card, so start from the label and walk up to the card with find_parent("li").

climb = get_soup(urljoin(BASE_URL, "category-climbing.html"))
low = []
for label in climb.select("p.stock.low-stock, p.stock.out-of-stock"):
    card = label.find_parent("li")
    low.append({"sku": card["data-sku"],
                "name": card.select_one("h2.product-name a").get_text(strip=True),
                "price": float(card.select_one("p.price")["data-price"]),
                "stock": label["class"][1]})
print(pd.DataFrame(low).to_string(index=False))
     sku                                 name  price        stock
CLM-1005        Larkspur Ridge Mesa Chalk Bag  28.95    low-stock
CLM-1010 Cedar Notch Tundra Locking Carabiner  12.50    low-stock
CLM-1014    Granite Fork Summit Quickdraw Set 133.95    low-stock
CLM-1022 Quillback Trailhead Climbing Harness  58.95 out-of-stock

         for tr in page.select("table.specs tr")}
print(specs)

6.3

Dynamic Pages with Selenium

Key ideas
  • Some pages arrive flat-packed: an empty frame, a data file, and JavaScript that assembles them. requests never runs the JavaScript.
  • Look for the data first: the Network tab (Fetch/XHR) or the page's script. If you may request the file, do that.
  • Drive a browser only when you must: the data is assembled in the page, it takes interaction to reveal, or you need what a person sees.
  • webdriver.Chrome(options=...) starts a real browser; Selenium Manager fetches the driver the first time.
  • find_element returns one element or raises; find_elements returns a list, maybe empty.
  • Never sleep. Wait by name: WebDriverWait(driver, 10).until(EC.presence_of_element_located((By.ID, "x"))), with the locator as one tuple.
  • Let Selenium render, then parse driver.page_source with Beautiful Soup.
  • A GET form is a slow way of building a URL: urlencode builds it directly.
  • driver.quit() in a finally block, every time.
ConditionTrue when
presence_of_element_locatedit exists
visibility_of_element_locatedit exists and shows
element_to_be_clickableit shows and is enabled
text_to_be_present_in_elementits text contains ...
url_containsthe address contains ...
Worked example: the search page, without a browser

Question: which packs cost $100 or less? The Search page answers it with JavaScript from products.json. Ask the file:

resp = requests.get(urljoin(BASE_URL, "products.json"), headers=HEADERS, timeout=10)
resp.raise_for_status()
shop = pd.DataFrame(resp.json()["products"])
hits = shop[(shop["category"] == "Packs") & (shop["price"] <= 100)]
print(len(hits), "products")
print(hits[["sku", "price", "stock_status"]].head(4).to_string(index=False))
11 products
     sku  price stock_status
PCK-1005  82.95     in_stock
PCK-1006  84.95     in_stock
PCK-1007  41.95     in_stock
PCK-1008  70.95     in_stock

Eleven packs, the same 11 the browser's form search reported ("11 products found"), with no browser, no waits and no form. Before you automate a page, ask where its data comes from.

Glossary

Element: one piece of HTML: a tag name, attributes (class, href, data-sku) and content.

Beautiful Soup: the library (bs4) that turns HTML into a tree you can search.

CSS selector: a pattern such as li.product-card p.price that names elements by tag, class, id or attribute.

select / select_one: every match as a list / the first match or None.

data- attribute: a machine-readable value a site stores for its own scripts, such as data-price="299.95".

Pagination: a listing split over pages, joined by "next" links.

read_html: pandas' reader for real <table> elements; it returns a list of DataFrames.

Dynamic page: a page whose content is added by JavaScript after it loads.

Selenium: a library that drives a real browser from Python.

Headless: a browser running without a window.

Explicit wait: WebDriverWait(driver, 10).until(condition): wait for a named condition, up to a limit.

WebElement: Selenium's handle on one live element; stale after the page changes.

page_source: the browser's current HTML, after scripts have run.

Instructor Kit · Participant Workbook

Session 18: APIs: Keys, Tokens and Polite Clients

Your pages for Session 18. Before the break: API keys, logins and tokens, kept out of your code. After the break: how to be a client a server is glad to have. Then Checkpoint 2: one database, one API, one honest answer.

Lessons 6.4–6.5 Session 18 · 3 hours Name: ____________________

Session Goals

  • keep keys and passwords out of code, output and logs
  • send an API key in a header and tell a 401 from a 403
  • log in for a bearer token, use it, and renew it once when it expires
  • follow next to the end of a paginated API and check the result
  • read rate-limit headers, pace requests, and honor 429 and Retry-After
  • retry only what is temporary, with backoff, jitter and a limit; cache responses; make long jobs resumable
  • show what you can do on your own in Checkpoint 2
The six ground rules (every Module 6 session starts here)
  1. Read robots.txt and obey it. It is the owner's published request to programs: where they may go and how fast.
  2. Read the terms of use. If they forbid automated collection, the answer is no, however easy the code would be.
  3. Prefer an API or a download when one exists. It is the front door.
  4. Go slowly and say who you are. Pause between requests; send a User-Agent with a way to contact you.
  5. Never go around a login, a paywall or a block. If you are asked to stop, stop.
  6. Do not collect personal data. Names, emails, profiles and photos of real people are protected by law in many places.

Lessons

6.4

API Authentication

Key ideas
  • Secrets live in the environment, not in code: os.environ.get("PINECREST_API_KEY"). Print a hint (pine...), never the value.
  • The key goes in the header the documentation names (X-API-Key), never in the URL.
  • 401: "I cannot tell who you are" (missing, malformed or expired credential). 403: "I understood, and the answer is no." Never retry a 403.
  • Turn error bodies into an exception that keeps status, code and message (ApiError), so code can decide by status what to do next.
  • Log in with requests.post(url, json={...}). Send the token as Authorization: Bearer <token>.
  • Tokens expire. On token_expired, log in again and repeat the request once; anything else surfaces.
  • Ask for one record first to learn the size of the job. Then follow next until it is None, sending the filters only with the first request.
  • Check what you collected: the promised total, unique ids, the date range, and the source when you have it.
StatusMeansYour program
400malformed requestfix the code
401no valid credentialrenew an expired token once; otherwise stop
403recognized, not allowedstop
404no such thingstop, or record "does not exist"
429too fastwait (Retry-After), retry
5xxthe server failedwait, retry a few times
Worked example: cancellations in the week before Christmas

Question: how many orders were cancelled from December 18 to December 24, 2025, and what were they worth? The API filters by since only, so ask for the size first, pull everything since the 18th, and cut the end date in pandas.

since = "2025-12-18"
probe = check(requests.get(f"{API}/orders", headers=KEY_HEADERS, timeout=10,
                           params={"status": "cancelled", "since": since,
                                   "per_page": 1})).json()
print(probe["total"], "cancelled orders since", since, "| pages at 5 per page:",
      -(-probe["total"] // 5))
14 cancelled orders since 2025-12-18 | pages at 5 per page: 3
week = pd.DataFrame(get_all("/orders", {"status": "cancelled", "since": since,
                                        "per_page": 5}))
week = week[week["order_date"] <= "2025-12-24"]
print(week[["order_id", "order_date", "store", "payment_method", "order_total"]]
      .to_string(index=False))
print("total value:", round(week["order_total"].sum(), 2))
 order_id order_date  store payment_method  order_total
   119840 2025-12-18 Online           card        53.90
   119860 2025-12-18 Online digital_wallet        62.90
   119900 2025-12-20 Online digital_wallet       176.40
   119921 2025-12-20 Online digital_wallet        93.90
   119991 2025-12-21 Online digital_wallet       317.65
   120039 2025-12-23 Online digital_wallet       194.35
   120071 2025-12-24 Online           card        48.85
total value: 947.95

Fourteen cancellations since December 18, seven of them in the week up to the 24th, all online, worth $947.95. Three requests, one probe, one filter in pandas.

6.5

Rate Limiting and Retries

Key ideas
  • A rate limit is the owner's rule: so many requests per window per client. X-RateLimit-Limit, -Remaining, -Reset tell you where you stand.
  • Plan A: pace yourself. Interval = window ÷ allowance × 1.1. A Throttle sleeps only for what is left.
  • Plan B: obey 429. Wait Retry-After seconds (with a default), retry, with a maximum number of tries.
  • Retry only what is temporary: 429, 500, 502, 503, 504, timeouts, dropped connections. Never 400, 403, 404.
  • Exponential backoff (0.5, 1, 2, 4, 8 s, capped) plus jitter (a random wait below that ceiling).
  • urllib3's Retry on an HTTPAdapter mounted on a Session does this for you; retry only GET and HEAD.
  • Cache: same request, same file. Check the status before writing, so errors are never cached.
  • Resumable jobs: one file per page, skip files that exist, write to .part then rename.
  • Log milestones and problems; never log a key, a token or a password.
Worked example: plan the job before you run it

Question: how long will a download take at a polite pace? Three services, three limits:

for limit, per, pages in [(10, 5, 21), (30, 10, 204), (5000, 3600, 100_000)]:
    gap = per / limit * 1.1
    print(f"{limit:>5} per {per:>4} s -> one every {gap:.2f} s; "
          f"{pages:>7,} requests: {pages * gap:>7,.0f} s ({pages * gap / 3600:.1f} h)")
   10 per    5 s -> one every 0.55 s;      21 requests:      12 s (0.0 h)
   30 per   10 s -> one every 0.37 s;     204 requests:      75 s (0.0 h)
 5000 per 3600 s -> one every 0.79 s; 100,000 requests:  79,200 s (22.0 h)

Twenty-one requests take twelve seconds; 100,000 requests at 5,000 an hour take 22 hours. Know that before you press run.

Glossary

API key: a long secret string that identifies your program to an API; sent in a header such as X-API-Key.

Environment variable: a named setting of the terminal session (PINECREST_API_KEY), read with os.environ.

401 Unauthorized: no valid credential: missing, malformed or expired.

403 Forbidden: the credential was understood and refused.

POST: the method that sends data in the request body; json= encodes a dictionary as JSON.

Bearer token: a short-lived credential from a login, sent as Authorization: Bearer <token>.

ApiError: the lesson's exception carrying status, code and message from the API's error body.

Rate limit: the owner's rule for how many requests one client may send per window.

429 Too Many Requests: over the limit; Retry-After says how long to wait.

Throttle: an object that keeps requests at least a set interval apart.

Exponential backoff: waiting longer after each failure (0.5, 1, 2, 4 s...), up to a cap.

Jitter: randomness added to backoff so clients do not retry in waves.

Cache: a saved copy of a response, used instead of asking again.

Resumable job: a download that saves each piece as it arrives and skips finished pieces on restart.

Lessons

7.1

Statistical Graphics with Seaborn

By the end you can
  • Draw scatter, line, histogram, ECDF, box, bar and point charts from a tidy table with one call each
  • Tell an axes-level function from a figure-level one and pick the right one
  • Split a chart with hue, col and row instead of a loop
  • Say what an error bar or band shows, and set it with errorbar=
  • Finish a seaborn chart with Matplotlib
Key ideas
  • Seaborn sits on Matplotlib. Every seaborn chart is a Matplotlib figure. You name the table and the roles: sns.something(data=table, x="col", y="col", hue="col").
  • Tidy in, chart out. One row per observation, one column per variable. If a chart is hard to describe, reshape with melt first.
  • Axes-level functions (scatterplot, lineplot, histplot, ecdfplot, boxplot, barplot, pointplot, heatmap) take ax= and return that Axes. Use them inside your own plt.subplots() layout.
  • Figure-level functions (relplot, displot, catplot) make their own figure, build panels from col= and row=, and return a FacetGrid. Size them with height= and aspect=.
  • Seaborn aggregates. lineplot, barplot and pointplot draw the mean of the rows that share an x, plus a bootstrapped 95% confidence interval. A band you did not expect means several rows per x.
  • Two kinds of error bar. Spread of the data ("sd", ("pi", 50)) or uncertainty of the estimate (("ci", 95), "se"). Uncertainty shrinks as the sample grows; spread does not. Always say which one you drew.
  • Order is your job. Categories appear in first-seen order. Pass order=, hue_order=, col_order=.
  • sns.set_theme(style="whitegrid", palette="colorblind") once, at the top. Pass seed= to make bootstrapped bars repeatable.
You want to...Write
One color per grouphue="store"
One panel per groupsns.relplot(..., col="store", col_wrap=2)
A chart inside my own layoutsns.barplot(..., ax=axes[0])
Bars that show spreaderrorbar="sd"
No bars at allerrorbar=None
A title on a FacetGridg.figure.suptitle("...") or g.set_titles("{col_name}")
Find out what I am holdingprint(type(obj).__name__)
Worked example: one line, two bands

The weather table has 731 days for each of the four store cities. The same lineplot call is drawn twice; only errorbar= changes. The printed table is July in numbers.

weather = pd.read_csv("data/store_weather.csv", parse_dates=["date"])
weather["month"] = weather["date"].dt.month
fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(10, 3.6), sharey=True, layout="constrained")
sns.lineplot(data=weather, x="month", y="high_f", hue="city",
             errorbar=("ci", 95), seed=42, ax=axes[0])
axes[0].set_title("Band: 95% CI of the mean", loc="left")
sns.lineplot(data=weather, x="month", y="high_f", hue="city",
             errorbar="sd", legend=False, ax=axes[1])
axes[1].set_title("Band: mean +/- 1 standard deviation", loc="left")
fig.savefig("highs_two_bands.png", dpi=150)
july = weather[weather["month"] == 7].groupby("city")["high_f"]
july = july.agg(n="count", mean="mean", sd="std", se="sem")
print(july.round(2))
            n   mean    sd    se
city                            
Asheville  62  82.81  7.48  0.95
Bend       62  82.21  7.51  0.95
Boise      62  92.00  8.73  1.11
Flagstaff  62  83.21  6.94  0.88

Read July in the table. Each city has 62 July days. The standard deviation is about 7 to 9 degrees: that is how much one July day differs from another, and it is the width of the right-hand band. The standard error is about 1 degree: that is how well we know the July average, and it is why the left-hand band is almost too thin to see. Same data, same line, two honest bands that answer different questions.

7.2

Interactive Charts with Plotly

By the end you can
  • Build line, scatter, bar, box and heatmap charts with one Plotly Express call each
  • Design what a reader sees on hover
  • Finish a figure with update_layout, update_xaxes, update_yaxes and update_traces
  • Add reference lines, shaded regions and annotations
  • Save a standalone HTML file and say what data is inside it
Key ideas
  • A figure is a description: traces (the data) plus a layout (titles, axes, legend). Python builds it; plotly.js draws it in a browser. Inspect it: fig.data[0].type, len(fig.data), fig.layout.title.text.
  • Nothing shows until you ask: fig.show() in a notebook, or fig.write_html("chart.html") and open the file.
  • Plotly Express follows the seaborn pattern with different words (table below).
  • `px.bar` and `px.line` do not aggregate. They draw one mark per row. Summarize with groupby first, then plot the summary.
  • Design the hover. hover_name= is the bold first line; hover_data={"revenue": ":$,.0f"} adds and formats columns; hovertemplate gives full control; hovermode="x unified" suits time series.
  • The title is still the message. Most readers look at the default view for a few seconds.
  • `write_html` choices: include_plotlyjs=True (default, about 4.7 MB, works offline) or "cdn" (a few KB, reader must be online).
  • The data is in the file. Every x, y and hover column is readable text inside the HTML. Aggregate first and pass only the columns the chart needs.
seabornPlotly Express
data=tablefirst argument
hue=color=
col=, row=, col_wrap=facet_col=, facet_row=, facet_col_wrap=
order=, hue_order=category_orders={"col": [...]}
axis labels set on the Axeslabels={"col": "Nice name"}
(no hover)hover_name=, hover_data=
barplot averages for youpx.bar never does: aggregate first
Worked example: aggregate first, then one call

How do people pay, online and in the stores? The orders table has 20,302 rows; the chart needs eight numbers. Count with pandas, turn counts into shares within each channel, and hand Plotly that small table.

orders = pd.read_csv("data/orders.csv")
mix = orders.groupby(["channel", "payment_method"], as_index=False).size()
mix["share"] = mix["size"] / mix.groupby("channel")["size"].transform("sum")
print(mix.round(3).to_string(index=False))
methods = ["card", "digital_wallet", "gift_card", "cash"]
fig = px.bar(mix, x="payment_method", y="share", color="channel", barmode="group",
             text_auto=".0%", category_orders={"payment_method": methods},
             labels={"share": "Share of orders", "payment_method": ""},
             title="Cash is a store-only habit; wallets are an online one")
fig.update_yaxes(tickformat=".0%")
fig.write_html("payment_mix.html", include_plotlyjs="cdn")
print(len(fig.data), "traces |", [len(t.x) for t in fig.data], "bars each")
channel payment_method  size  share
 online           card  7953  0.619
 online digital_wallet  3876  0.302
 online      gift_card  1025  0.080
  store           card  4439  0.596
  store           cash  1614  0.217
  store digital_wallet   918  0.123
  store      gift_card   477  0.064
2 traces | [3, 4] bars each

Seven rows went in (cash does not exist online), so the figure has two traces with three bars (online) and four (store). The file payment_mix.html holds those seven shares and nothing else: no order ids, no customers. text_auto=".0%" prints each share on its bar, and category_orders puts the methods in the order you chose instead of the order of the rows.

Glossary

  • Tidy (long) table: one row per observation, one column per variable: the shape seaborn and Plotly Express expect.
  • Semantic mapping: giving a column a role in the chart: x, y, hue (color), size, style.
  • Axes-level function: a seaborn function that draws on one Matplotlib Axes (takes ax=, returns the Axes).
  • Figure-level function: relplot, displot or catplot: makes its own figure and returns a FacetGrid.
  • FacetGrid: seaborn's object for a grid of panels; g.figure and g.axes reach the Matplotlib parts.
  • Small multiples: the same chart repeated once per group with shared axes.
  • Estimator: the statistic a chart draws for each group; the mean unless you change it.
  • Confidence interval: a range for an estimate such as a mean; it narrows as the sample grows.
  • Bootstrap: resampling your own rows with replacement many times to see how much an estimate moves.
  • ECDF: empirical cumulative distribution: the share of values at or below each x; no bins to choose.
  • Trace: one data series in a Plotly figure (a line, a set of bars, a set of points).
  • Layout: everything in a Plotly figure that is not data: title, axes, legend, shapes, annotations.
  • Hover template: the small text pattern, with placeholders such as %{y:$,.0f}, that fills the hover label.
  • CDN: a public server the reader's browser downloads plotly.js from when the file was written with include_plotlyjs="cdn".
  • Kaleido: the helper package write_image uses to turn a Plotly figure into a PNG, SVG or PDF; it needs Chrome.

7.3

Interactive Charts with Bokeh

By the end you can
  • Build a figure by adding glyphs and save it with output_file and save
  • Put data in a ColumnDataSource and name its columns in glyph calls
  • Write a hover tool with formatted fields
  • Link plots by a shared range (panning) and by a shared source (brushing)
  • Add a dropdown or slider that works in a plain HTML file
Key ideas
  • Two moves: p = figure(...) makes an empty plot; p.line, p.scatter, p.vbar add glyphs. Each glyph call adds one renderer.
  • Everything is an object with properties: p.yaxis.formatter, p.legend.location, p.title.text.
  • `output_file(name)` then `save(p)` writes the HTML; show(p) also opens it in a browser.
  • A `ColumnDataSource` is the table behind the page. With source=source, a string argument is a column name; a number is the same value for every mark.
  • Hover: ("Revenue", "@revenue{$0,0}"). @name is this row's value; the format goes in braces. Dates need formatters={"@date": "datetime"}.
  • Share a range, share the view: figure(x_range=top.x_range). Share a source, share the rows: pass the same source variable to both glyphs and add box_select.
  • In a saved file only JavaScript can react: js_link copies a widget value into a property; CustomJS runs a few lines. A Python callback needs a server.
  • Every column of every source travels in the file. Build the source from a table that holds only what the page needs. A new page needs new objects (a source belongs to one document).
You want to...Write
Dollars on an axisp.yaxis.formatter = NumeralTickFormatter(format="$0,0")
Color by a category columncolor=factor_cmap("store", palette=..., factors=...)
Plots side by side / stackedrow(a, b) / column(a, b)
A selection that stands outnonselection_alpha=0.15, selection_color="#d97706"
Show only one groupview=CDSView(filter=GroupFilter(column_name="store", group="Boise"))
A file that works offlineoutput_file(name, mode="inline")
Find a blank plot's causeprint(source.column_names) and read the terminal for E-1001
Worked example: a source, bars and a formatted hover

Asheville is the rainy store city. Summarize two years of weather to twelve rows, put them in a source, and draw bars whose hover shows the share as a percentage. The source holds three columns besides the index, and nothing else travels in the file.

import pandas as pd
from bokeh.models import ColumnDataSource, NumeralTickFormatter
from bokeh.plotting import figure, output_file, save

weather = pd.read_csv("data/store_weather.csv", parse_dates=["date"])
ash = weather[weather["city"] == "Asheville"].copy()
ash["month"] = ash["date"].dt.strftime("%b")
ash["rainy"] = ash["precip_in"] >= 0.10
months = ["Jan", "Feb", "Mar", "Apr", "May", "Jun",
          "Jul", "Aug", "Sep", "Oct", "Nov", "Dec"]
table = ash.groupby("month").agg(high=("high_f", "mean"), rainy=("rainy", "mean"))
table = table.loc[months].reset_index()
source = ColumnDataSource(table)
tips = [("Month", "@month"), ("Rainy days", "@rainy{0%}"),
        ("Mean high", "@high{0} F")]
p = figure(width=700, height=300, x_range=months, tools="save", tooltips=tips,
           title="Asheville: July is the rainiest month")
p.vbar(x="month", top="rainy", width=0.8, source=source, color="#667eea")
p.yaxis.formatter = NumeralTickFormatter(format="0%")
p.y_range.start = 0
output_file("asheville_rain.html", title="Asheville rain")
save(p)
print(source.column_names, "|", len(p.renderers), "renderer,", len(table), "bars")
print(table.round(2).iloc[[0, 5, 6, 7, 11]].to_string(index=False))
['index', 'month', 'high', 'rainy'] | 1 renderer, 12 bars
month  high  rainy
  Jan 47.87   0.21
  Jun 84.33   0.28
  Jul 82.81   0.39
  Aug 83.39   0.34
  Dec 51.32   0.19

One vbar call drew twelve bars and added one renderer. The strings "month" and "rainy" are column names in the source; width=0.8 is a plain number, the same for every bar. The tooltip reads @rainy{0%} from the same row. Open asheville_rain.html and hover July: 39% of July days had at least a tenth of an inch of rain.

7.4

Dashboards with Streamlit

By the end you can
  • Write an app as a plain script and start it with streamlit run
  • Explain the rerun model and predict what a variable holds after a click
  • Keep values with st.session_state and avoid repeated work with @st.cache_data
  • Lay out a page with a sidebar, columns and tabs
  • Test an app without a browser, and say what sharing one takes
Key ideas
  • An app is a script. Start it with streamlit run app.py from the folder that holds data/. Stop it with Ctrl+C. Not python app.py, and not in a notebook.
  • The whole script reruns from the top every time a reader changes a widget. Ordinary variables start over. Widgets keep their values.
  • Showing and asking. st.title, st.metric, st.dataframe, st.plotly_chart show things. st.selectbox, st.multiselect, st.slider, st.date_input, st.toggle ask, and return the answer.
  • `st.button` is True for one rerun only. It is not a switch.
  • `st.session_state` is a dictionary kept per browser tab across reruns. A widget with key="x" lives there as st.session_state["x"]. Change a widget's value only in a callback (on_click=).
  • `@st.cache_data` stores a function's result per set of arguments. Use it for slow loading that does not depend on widgets. The cache is shared by all users.
  • `st.stop()` ends the current run after a message: for an empty selection or a half-chosen date range.
  • Sharing means a Python process kept running where readers can reach it, with the packages, the data, access control and no secrets in the code.
Where a value livesHow long it lasts
an ordinary variableone run of the script
a widget's valueacross reruns, in that browser tab
st.session_state["x"]across reruns, in that browser tab
a @st.cache_data resultfor every user, until the app restarts or the cache is cleared
Filters / side by side / viewswith st.sidebar: / st.columns(3) / st.tabs([...])
Test without a browserAppTest.from_file("app.py").run() then assert not at.exception
Worked example: a small app and its test

How many days count as rainy depends on where you draw the line. This app lets the reader choose the city and the line. The loading is cached; the filtering is not, so it always reflects the widgets.

File rain_app.py:

import pandas as pd
import streamlit as st

@st.cache_data
def load_weather(path):
    return pd.read_csv(path, parse_dates=["date"])

weather = load_weather("data/store_weather.csv")
city = st.selectbox("City", ["Boise", "Bend", "Flagstaff", "Asheville"])
inches = st.slider("A rainy day has at least this many inches",
                   0.05, 1.00, 0.10, step=0.05)
one = weather[weather["city"] == city]
rainy = one["precip_in"] >= inches
st.metric("Rainy days", f"{rainy.mean():.0%}",
          help="Share of the 731 days in 2024 and 2025")
st.caption(f"{int(rainy.sum())} of {len(one)} days in {city}")

Check it without a browser:

from streamlit.testing.v1 import AppTest

def report():
    print(at.selectbox[0].value, at.slider[0].value, "->", at.metric[0].value,
          "|", at.caption[0].value)

at = AppTest.from_file("rain_app.py", default_timeout=30).run()
report()
at.selectbox[0].select("Asheville").run()
report()
at.slider[0].set_value(0.5).run()
report()
Boise 0.1 -> 9% | 68 of 731 days in Boise
Asheville 0.1 -> 24% | 172 of 731 days in Asheville
Asheville 0.5 -> 6% | 44 of 731 days in Asheville

Three runs of the script, three different pages. AppTest ran the script in memory, changed a widget and ran it again, exactly as a reader's click would. Boise is rainy on about one day in ten at the default line; Asheville on about one in four; raise the line to half an inch and Asheville's share falls to 6%. No value was carried from one run to the next except the widgets' own.

Before Session 21: capstone milestones 1 to 4
  • ☐ Milestone 1 · Question and plan (30 min). The paragraph above becomes the first Markdown cell of pinecrest_report.ipynb. Done when someone else could read it and predict roughly what the notebook will contain.
  • ☐ Milestone 2 · Load and check (45 min). For every file: shape, dtypes, date range, missing values, duplicate keys. Done when you can say what one row is and how many rows there are.
  • ☐ Milestone 3 · Clean and prepare (45 min). One function returns your analysis table, with a row-count check around every merge and filter. Done when you can explain every row that was dropped.
  • ☐ Milestone 4 · Explore (75 min). One variable, then pairs, then segments; a running list headed "What I have noticed". Done when you can say in two sentences what you expect the answer to be and what could still prove you wrong.
  • ☐ Restart and Run All works on what I have so far.

  • ☐ For Session 21: my capstone notebook runs from a fresh kernel as far as I have got

Glossary

  • Glyph: a family of marks added to a Bokeh figure: line, scatter, vbar.
  • Renderer: what one glyph call adds to a figure; p.renderers lists them.
  • ColumnDataSource (CDS): the table behind a Bokeh page: a dictionary of equal-length columns.
  • Linked panning: two plots that share a range object, so they always show the same view.
  • Linked brushing: two plots that share a source, so a selection in one highlights the same rows in the other.
  • View (CDSView): a filter between a source and a glyph that shows only some rows.
  • CustomJS: a few lines of JavaScript attached to a widget or property, run in the reader's browser.
  • Rerun: one top-to-bottom run of a Streamlit script, triggered by opening the page or changing a widget.
  • Widget: a control (selectbox, slider, button) whose function returns the reader's input.
  • Session state: st.session_state: a dictionary Streamlit keeps for one browser tab across reruns.
  • Callback: a function passed as on_click= or on_change=; Streamlit runs it before the rerun.
  • Cache: stored results of a function decorated with @st.cache_data, reused when the arguments repeat.
  • AppTest: Streamlit's testing tool: runs an app in memory and lets code read the page and operate widgets.
  • Deploying: keeping an app running on a machine other people can reach.

Lesson

8.1

The Pinecrest Report

By the end of today you can
  • State your main result as one sentence with an estimate, an interval, a test and an effect size
  • Try to break your own result with one robustness check
  • Draw one figure that carries your answer on its own
  • Present your answer in three minutes, and finish the report within a week
Key ideas
  • Compare like with like. Before you believe a difference, ask what else differs between the groups: the season, the time each customer had, the weekdays, the products.
  • Fit the method to the data. Two proportions: a z-test with Cohen's h. A skewed amount: medians with a bootstrap interval, means alongside. Several groups: one overall test first, then a small number of planned pairs, adjusted.
  • Five parts in every claim: the estimates, the difference, the 95% interval, the test, the effect size.
  • An honest null is a full answer. Say what the interval rules out: "no difference larger than about X".
  • Words about cause ("causes", "drives", "because") only where the design supports them.
  • One figure, one message. A title that states the finding, axes with units, error bars named in the caption, saved from code.
  • If it does not rerun from a fresh start, it is not finished.
Worked example: a strong sentence for a null result

A practice question (not a capstone question): do customers who opted in to marketing recommend Pinecrest more? "Promoter" means a recommend score of 9 or 10.

from statsmodels.stats.proportion import confint_proportions_2indep, proportions_ztest

survey = pd.read_csv("data/survey.csv")
customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers.csv")
t = survey.merge(customers[["customer_id", "marketing_opt_in"]], on="customer_id",
                 validate="one_to_one").dropna(subset=["recommend"])
t["promoter"] = t["recommend"] >= 9
g = t.groupby("marketing_opt_in")["promoter"].agg(["sum", "count"])
yes, no = g.loc[True], g.loc[False]
p1, p0 = yes["sum"] / yes["count"], no["sum"] / no["count"]
z, p = proportions_ztest([yes["sum"], no["sum"]], [yes["count"], no["count"]])
low, high = confint_proportions_2indep(yes["sum"], yes["count"], no["sum"], no["count"])
h = 2 * np.arcsin(np.sqrt(p1)) - 2 * np.arcsin(np.sqrt(p0))
print(f"Promoters (recommend 9 or 10): {p1:.1%} of opted-in customers (n={yes['count']}) "
      f"against {p0:.1%} of the others (n={no['count']}), a difference of {(p1 - p0) * 100:+.1f} points "
      f"(95% CI {low * 100:+.1f} to {high * 100:+.1f}; z-test p = {p:.2f}; h = {h:.3f}).")
Promoters (recommend 9 or 10): 32.3% of opted-in customers (n=1020) against 31.3% of the others (n=726), a difference of +1.0 points (95% CI -3.5 to +5.4; z-test p = 0.66; h = 0.021).

Every part is there: two rates with their group sizes, the difference, the interval, the test and the effect size. The interval runs from about 3.5 points lower to 5.4 points higher: the data cannot tell the groups apart, and they rule out a difference much larger than 5 points. That is the sentence a report would carry, word for word.

Submission Checklist (within one week)

  • ☐ pinecrest_report.ipynb: Restart and Run All succeeds from the project folder; relative paths; seeds set
  • ☐ One or two figures, saved by the notebook, each with a finding-first title
  • ☐ report.md: one page, Answer first, specific Caveats, Next questions, three-line Method
  • ☐ README.md: what this is, how to run it, where the data is from (fictional course data), and what help I used (AI assistants, web code, classmates)

  • ☐ Every number in the report can be found in the notebook's output
  • ☐ The self-review checklist from Lesson 8.1, every box ticked honestly

Notes

Glossary

  • Unit of analysis: what one row of your analysis table is: a store-day, a customer, a product.
  • Fair comparison: groups that differ in the thing you care about and, as far as you can arrange, in nothing else.
  • Follow-up window: a fixed period after an event (for example 180 days) in which an outcome is counted, the same for everyone.
  • Difference in differences: the change in an affected group minus (or divided by) the change in an unaffected group over the same period.
  • Effect size: how big a difference is, on a scale that does not depend on sample size: Cohen's d for means, h for proportions.
  • Multiple comparisons: running many tests; some will look significant by chance, so plan the main one and adjust or report the rest.
  • Honest null: a carefully measured "no clear difference", reported with the size of difference the data rule out.
  • Robustness check: repeating the main comparison with another reasonable definition or subset to see whether it holds.
  • Reproducible: the notebook gives the same numbers after Restart and Run All, on another machine, from relative paths.
